A model for mosquito-borne epidemic outbreaks with information-dependent protective behaviour
Authors: Simone De Reggi, Andrea Pugliese, Mattia Sensi, Cinzia Soresina
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We investigate a model for a mosquito-borne epidemic in which human hosts may adopt protective behaviour against vector bites in response to information on both past and current disease prevalence. Assuming that mosquitoes can also feed on non-competent hosts (i.e. hosts that do not contribute to disease transmission), we first revisit existing results and show that behaviour-driven protection may either decrease or increase the basic reproduction number, depending on the interaction between behavioural response, host composition, and transmission parameters. Assuming that opinion dynamics evolves on a much faster time scale than disease transmission, we then apply Geometric Singular Perturbation Theory to effectively reduce the original two-group model to a model for a homogeneous host population. The reduced system enables a detailed investigation of the impact of information-induced behavioural changes on the transient dynamics of the epidemic, including scenarios in which protective measures lead to outbreaks with low attack rates. Our analysis shows that behavioural responses may either facilitate epidemic control or prolong disease persistence, potentially generating recurrent damped epidemic waves. Numerical simulations are provided to illustrate and support the analytical findings.

Introduction

In recent years, there has been growing concern over the rise in mosquito-borne diseases in both endemic and non-endemic areas (European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control 2025; World Health Organization 2025). Malaria, dengue, chikungunya, Japanese encephalitis, West Nile virus, yellow fever, and Zika are among the most prominent examples of mosquito-borne infections. These diseases are transmitted to humans through the bites of infected mosquitoes carrying pathogens (viruses or other parasites), and represent major threats to global public health (World Health Organization 2025). Factors such as climate change, globalisation, and urbanisation have strongly favoured the spread of many of these infections, such as dengue and chikungunya, to non-tropical areas, creating numerous new habitats suitable for mosquito life (Chala and Hamde 2021).

Mathematical models play a crucial role in understanding the spread of mosquito-borne infections and in assessing the potential impact of control strategies. The classical Ross–Macdonald framework provides the foundation for much of the mathematical modelling of such diseases; see, for instance, the recent review by Pugliese and De Reggi (2026). Originally proposed by Ronald Ross between 1908 and 1911 to investigate the effect of control measures on malaria transmission (Ross 1911), the model was later extended by George Macdonald to incorporate, among other features, incubation periods, i.e. time delays between infection and infectiousness (Macdonald 1957) (the role of delays had already been considered by Sharpe and Lotka (1923); however, they neglected mosquito mortality during the incubation period. Moreover, Macdonald acknowledged Armitage (1953) for mathematical ideas related to delays; see Pugliese and De Reggi (2026), Smith et al. (2012)). In particular, the mathematical modelling of dengue, which strongly motivates the present work, poses significant epidemiological and methodological challenges; see the recent review Aguiar et al. (2022).

A key feature distinguishing vector-borne infections from directly transmitted ones is that epidemic containment can be achieved through interventions targeting the vector population (Ogunlade et al. 2023). This insight was already recognised by Ross in his pioneering work on malaria, where he developed arguments that anticipated the modern concept of the Basic Reproduction Number (BRN), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document}R0; see Bacaër (2011), Heesterbeek (2002), Pugliese and De Reggi (2026). However, effective control of mosquito-borne epidemics generally requires combining vector-targeted interventions with preventive measures adopted by humans, such as the use of repellents or bed nets.

Theoretical investigations of the effectiveness of such strategies require mathematical models incorporating behavioural components. As clearly illustrated by the COVID-19 pandemic, human behaviour can substantially influence epidemic spread, since individuals may dynamically adjust their actions in response to available information. For example, individuals may decide whether to be vaccinated or to adopt self-protective measures, either in accordance with public health guidelines or on their own initiative. The behavioural epidemiology of infectious diseases addresses these aspects by incorporating behavioural dynamics into mathematical models of disease transmission (Manfredi and d’Onofrio 2013). Its origins can be traced back to the seminal work of Capasso and Serio (1978), who first proposed an SIR (Susceptible–Infectious–Removed) model in which the transmission rate was assumed to decrease as a function of the current prevalence, that is, the total number of infected individuals (d’Onofrio and Manfredi 2009). Since then, numerous approaches have been developed to incorporate behavioural feedback into epidemic models. We refer to the monograph by Manfredi and d’Onofrio (2013) and to the recent reviews by Bedson et al. (2021), Funk et al. (2010), Wang et al. (2016) for comprehensive overviews of this field.

Although a substantial body of literature now addresses the interplay between the spread of directly transmitted infections and human behaviour, comparatively less attention has been devoted to information-induced behavioural effects in vector-borne diseases. In the following paragraphs, we briefly review selected contributions in this area.

For directly transmitted infections, the adoption of preventive behaviour by a subset of individuals generally reduces contact rates across the population, thereby producing an overall beneficial effect. Mathematically, this is typically modelled by assuming that transmission rates decrease as functions of the level of awareness within the population (Manfredi and d’Onofrio 2013). In contrast, for vector-borne infections, modelling partial protective behaviour among hosts is far from straightforward, as predictions regarding its effectiveness critically depend on the assumptions governing vector–host ecological interactions (McCallum et al. 2001; Thongsripong et al. 2021). In particular, different assumptions on how mosquito biting rates depend on host availability may lead to substantially different predictions concerning the probability of epidemic invasion and the effectiveness of control strategies (Demers et al. 2018); see also Wonham et al. (2006).

As an example, for vector-borne infections, the adoption of self-protective measures by a subset of individuals may divert mosquito bites towards unprotected hosts (Killeen and Smith 2007; Moore et al. 2007), thereby increasing the likelihood of mosquito–host–mosquito transmission cycles (Demers et al. 2018; Killeen and Smith 2007; Miller et al. 2016). More generally, partially adopted protective behaviour may enhance host heterogeneity, as individuals using repellents or bed nets become less attractive to mosquitoes than those who remain unprotected (Killeen and Smith 2007; Miller et al. 2016).

Dye and Hasibeder (1986) showed that host heterogeneity in vector-borne epidemics may, perhaps counter-intuitively, increase the value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document}R0, thereby enhancing the invasion potential of the disease. At the same time, they demonstrated that the final epidemic size may decrease under such heterogeneity (Hasibeder and Dye 1988). This phenomenon was revisited by Miller and Huppert (2013), who considered a simple vector–host model with two host classes and investigated the effects of host diversity, transmission competence, and vector preference on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document}R0. In a subsequent work, Miller et al. (2016) analysed a one-vector–one-host model in which the host population is statically divided into two subgroups: individuals adopting protective measures and individuals who do not. They found that, depending on parameter values, the use of protective measures by only a fraction of the host population may increase \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document}R0, thereby exacerbating the risk of epidemic invasion. On the other hand, if protective measures such as bed nets or repellents significantly increase the time mosquitoes spend attempting to bite protected individuals, then a beneficial effect may also extend to the unprotected subgroup.

Recently, Demers et al. (2018) investigated, primarily through numerical simulations and analysis of the basic reproduction number, the potential effects of behavioural changes during the early phase of a mosquito-borne outbreak, including limiting cases of very small or very large behavioural switching rates. The authors showed numerically that incorporating behavioural changes can lead to a lower \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document}R0 compared with a model with static behaviour. Building on this framework, subsequent studies (Brito Da Cruz and Rodrigues 2021; Roosa and Fefferman 2022) have proposed models of increasing complexity to explore more elaborate scenarios involving behavioural adaptation. However, most of these contributions rely predominantly on numerical simulations and provide limited analytical insight into the impact of (possibly information-induced) protective behaviour on epidemic spread, beyond considerations based on the basic reproduction number.

The idea of information-dependent behavioural switching in vector–host models has also been explored in Misra et al. (2013) and, more recently, in Hu and Nie (2023), where it is assumed that only the susceptible host subpopulation is divided into protected (with perfect protection) and unprotected individuals, with behavioural changes driven by exponentially waning information on infection prevalence. Incorporating host demography and thus focusing on an endemic setting, the authors showed that an endemic equilibrium exists whenever \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0>1$$\end{document}R0>1 and may become unstable via a Hopf bifurcation under suitable conditions, leading to the emergence of periodic solutions. The possibility of oscillatory, or even more complex, dynamics in epidemic models with information-induced behavioural responses has been widely discussed in the literature; see, for instance, Andò et al. (2026), d’Onofrio and Manfredi (2009), d’Onofrio and Manfredi (2022), Manfredi and d’Onofrio (2013), Poletti et al. (2009), Zhang et al. (2023).

Motivated by the recent rise of autochthonous dengue cases in Europe (European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control 2025), we propose a model for a mosquito-borne outbreak in which host individuals may adopt protective behaviour in response to the information available on the current state of the epidemic. Protective behaviour may consist, e.g. in using repellents, wearing long-sleeved clothes, using screens on windows and doors, or using air conditioning (we neglect possible deadly effects on the vector population deriving from the use of such measures); see Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (2026). In contrast to Misra et al. (2013), Hu and Nie (2023), we focus on an outbreak scenario and therefore neglect host demography, waning immunity, and seasonal effects in the vector population. In this outbreak scenario, the epidemic will certainly go extinct; see, e.g. Propositions 6 and 10 below. Hence, the focus of our analysis is on characterising the transient dynamics before extinction. Following Miller et al. (2016), we assume that the host population is divided into protected and unprotected subclasses, with individuals dynamically switching behaviour as in Demers et al. (2018) at rates depending on epidemic information. To model this mechanism, we adopt the information index approach introduced by d’Onofrio, Manfredi, and collaborators, whereby the delayed influence of past infections on current perception is represented through a memory kernel, which may be either distributed or concentrated (Andò et al. 2020). This information may depend on prevalence (d’Onofrio and Manfredi 2009; d’Onofrio et al. 2007) or on incidence, namely the number of new cases per unit time (d’Onofrio and Manfredi 2022). Consistently, with the structure of the resulting system of Ordinary Differential Equations (ODEs), we assume that the human population is closed and constant in size, with no births or deaths. Furthermore, we allow vectors to feed on a non-human host population that does not contribute to transmission (Esteva and Vargas 1998; Nishiura 2006). For instance, field studies in Italy found that Aedes albopictus (main vector of dengue and chikungunya in Europe) feeds predominantly on humans in urban settings (79–96% of blood meals), whereas in rural environments humans account for only 23–55%, with horses and bovines representing common alternative hosts (Valerio et al. 2010).

In the literature, it is usual to assume that epidemiological dynamics evolves either much more slowly or much more rapidly than behavioural changes in the host population; see, for example, Della Marca and Onofrio (2021), Della Marca et al. (2024), Manfredi and d’Onofrio (2013), Poletti et al. (2009). This is often motivated by the increasing role that social media play in accelerating opinion shifts and decision dynamics in different contexts, such as the prevention of infectious diseases (via, e.g. non-pharmaceutical interventions, such as social distancing, or vaccination campaigns). Indeed, several studies (e.g. Bolton et al. (2024), Urmi et al. (2025)) show that, during the COVID-19 pandemic, the number of contacts and other behaviours changed quickly in response to changes in the law or in the observed mortality. When such models are formulated as systems of ODEs, a powerful framework for analysing the qualitative behaviour under time-scale separation is the so-called Geometric Singular Perturbation Theory (GSPT), originating from the seminal work of Neil Fenichel (Fenichel 1979). GSPT has been widely applied to the study of natural systems characterised by interacting mechanisms evolving on distinct time scales; see, in particular, Hek (2010), Kuehn (2015) for biologically motivated examples. Under the assumption of separated time scales, perturbed ODE systems can be analysed in suitable singular limits that describe the fast and slow dynamics separately, and the resulting reduced problems provide insight into the behaviour of the full system. This approach has been employed in several epidemic models (Jardón-Kojakhmetov et al. 2021a, b; Kaklamanos et al. 2024), especially in settings where information (as well as misinformation and opinions) spreads faster than the disease itself (Bulai et al. 2024; Della Marca et al. 2024; Schecter 2021).

In this work, we exploit GSPT to understand both the transient and the asymptotic behaviour of the system. In Sect. 4, we rigorously prove that the model in which individuals switch between protected and unprotected behaviours can be approximated, as the switching rates become very large, by model (38), in which only one type of hosts is present, but infection rate depends on the information index. In Sect. 5, we use another asymptotic analysis on model (38) that allows us to explain a slow decay in the infectious population, which is uncommon for compartmental models without behaviour dynamics, and which we observe in our base behavioural model (22), as well. It is worth noting that behavioural and epidemiological processes are not the only mechanisms operating on different time scales. In mosquito-borne diseases, demographic processes in the vector population typically occur on the same time scale as epidemic transmission and therefore cannot be neglected, whereas demographic changes in the human population, as well as waning disease-induced immunity, often evolve much more slowly and may be disregarded in early outbreak scenarios.

The paper is organised as follows. In Sect. 2, we introduce the baseline vector-borne model without information and analyse its main properties and asymptotic behaviour. Section 3 incorporates information-induced behavioural changes through an information index based on a memory kernel; specialising to Erlang-distributed kernels, we derive the corresponding reproduction number. In Sect. 4, we further assume a separation of time scales and apply GSPT to investigate the transient dynamics of the system and present numerical simulations illustrating the analytical results in epidemiologically relevant scenarios. In Sect. 5, we examine the model towards the end of a first outbreak, thus assuming a low attack rate. We investigate, for selected memory kernels, the existence and stability of equilibria of the reduced system; these correspond to a slow decline of incidence in the original model. Finally, Sect. 6 summarises the main findings and outlines possible extensions of the model.

Vector-host model with static protective behaviour

In this section, we introduce the baseline model (a slight extension of the Ross–Macdonald framework incorporating non-human hosts). This model will later be extended to account for a division of the human population into individuals who adopt protective measures against mosquito bites and those who do not. At this stage, behaviour is assumed to be fixed. In the following section, we will allow individuals, irrespective of their epidemiological status, to switch dynamically between behavioural states. We remark that, while our model is motivated by recent outbreaks of vector-borne diseases such as dengue or chikungunya, the analysis carried out in this work does not aim at closely reproducing the dynamics of complex illnesses such as malaria or dengue. Rather, our goal is to introduce a base model which provides a useful starting point and a remarkable trade-off between modelling complexity and analytical tractability.

Let H denote the total human population size. We assume H is constant over time (a closed population with no births or deaths). We also assume that the vector can take blood meals from a non-human host population whose individuals do not participate in transmission, i.e. a bite on a non-human host is “wasted” from the standpoint of infection spread. We will refer to L as the “effective” population size of “non-competent” hosts; thus, L/(H+L)L/(H+L) is the probability that a mosquito bite falls on a “non-competent” host, and H/(H+L)H/(H+L) is the probability that it falls on a human host.

As for the epidemiological dynamics, we assume an SIR–SI framework. Humans follow an SIR model without demography, i.e. we assume no births or deaths, and permanent immunity after recovery. Mosquitoes follow an SI model with demography since their average lifespan is orders of magnitude shorter than that of humans. For simplicity, we neglect the incubation periods in both mosquitoes and hosts, during which individuals are infected but not yet infectious. It is well-known that including the extrinsic incubation period (the one in mosquitoes) reduces the value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document}R0, while having only minor effects on the qualitative dynamics of the system (Macdonald 1957; Pugliese and De Reggi 2026).

Thus, in the absence of protective behaviour, the dynamics is described by the following system of ODEs (Esteva and Vargas 1998; Nishiura 2006):1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{array}{rl} S_H' & = -bp_{H\leftarrow M}I_M \frac{S_H}{H+L},\\ I_H' & = bp_{H\leftarrow M}I_M \frac{S_{H} }{H+L} - \gamma I_{H} ,\\ R_H'& =\gamma I_H,\\ S_M' & = \Lambda -bp_{M\leftarrow H}S_M \frac{I_{H} }{H+L}-\mu S_M,\\ I_M' & = bp_{M\leftarrow H}S_M \frac{I_{H}}{H+L} -\mu I_M, \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}SH′=-bpH←MIMSHH+L,IH′=bpH←MIMSHH+L-γIH,RH′=γIH,SM′=Λ-bpM←HSMIHH+L-μSM,IM′=bpM←HSMIHH+L-μIM,where we denote with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_H(t),\, I_H(t),\, R_H(t)$$\end{document}SH(t),IH(t),RH(t) the number of human individuals who are susceptible, infectious, and removed, respectively, at time t≥ 0t≥0, and with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_M(t),\, I_M(t)$$\end{document}SM(t),IM(t) the number of mosquitoes who are susceptible and infectious, respectively, at time t≥ 0t≥0. The parameter b is the constant per-capita mosquito biting rate, assumed to be independent of the mosquito’s infection status. The quantities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{H\leftarrow M},\, p_{M \leftarrow H}\in [0, 1]$$\end{document}pH←M,pM←H∈[0,1] are the probabilities of transmission per bite from mosquitoes to humans and from humans to mosquitoes, respectively. The parameters Λ Λ and μ μ are the mosquito recruitment rate and per-capita death rate, respectively (hence the average mosquito lifespan is 1/μ 1/μ).

For the mosquito population dynamics, we assume for simplicity that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda = \mu M$$\end{document}Λ=μM. Hence, the equation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_H$$\end{document}RH can be omitted since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_H(t)\equiv H-S_H(t)-I_H(t)$$\end{document}RH(t)≡H-SH(t)-IH(t), for all t≥ 0t≥0.

Remark 1

Recall that L represents non-human targets for mosquito bites. An alternative interpretation of the constant L is a “penalty term” in the expression for the mean time mosquitoes spend searching for human hosts; for instance, it may reflect the spatial separation among humans. Then, by defining \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta :=L^{-1}$$\end{document}ζ:=L-1 and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{b}:=\frac{b H}{H+L}=\frac{b\zeta H}{1+\zeta H}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}b^:=bHH+L=bζH1+ζH,we can interpret \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{b}$$\end{document}b^ as the effective mosquito biting rate, determined by a Holling-type II functional response, as in Demers et al. (2018), Miller et al. (2016), Yakob (2016). The force of infection acting on humans can then be written as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda _{H\leftarrow M}:=\hat{b}\ {p_{H\leftarrow M}} \frac{I_M}{H}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λH←M:=b^pH←MIMH.In summary, L can be viewed as accounting for the effect on the average mosquito searching time of both the presence of non-competent hosts and the spatial distribution (or mutual distance) of humans.

Protective behaviour

To incorporate protective behaviour into model (1), following recent works in the literature (Demers et al. 2018; Miller et al. 2016; Roosa and Fefferman 2022), we assume that the host population H can be partitioned as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H=H_{P}+H_{NP}$$\end{document}H=HP+HNP. Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{P}=pH$$\end{document}HP=pH, with p∈ [0,1]p∈[0,1], represents the subpopulation of protected individuals, i.e. those adopting protective behaviour, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{NP}=(1-p)H$$\end{document}HNP=(1-p)H represents those who do not (the subpopulation of non-protected individuals).

To model the efficacy of protective behaviour, we assume that protected individuals are relatively less exposed to mosquito bites compared to non-protected individuals. Let q∈ [0, 1]q∈[0,1] denote the probability of protection failure (see, for instance, Roosa and Fefferman (2022)). The probability that a mosquito bites a protected individual in the group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{P}$$\end{document}HP is2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P_b^{H_P}(p,q)=\frac{q H_{P}}{q H_{P}+H_{NP}+L}=\frac{pqH}{c(p, q)H+L}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PbP(p,q)=qHPqHP+HNP+L=pqHc(p,q)H+L,where3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} c(p,q):=1-p(1-q). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}c(p,q):=1-p(1-q).Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_b^{H_P}(p,q)< P_b^{H_P}(p,1)$$\end{document}PbP(p,q)<PbP(p,1), for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\in (0,1),\ q\in [0,1)$$\end{document}p∈(0,1),q∈[0,1), i.e. the probability that an individual is bitten by a mosquito is effectively smaller if they adopt effective protection measures. Conversely, for the probability that a mosquito bites a non-protected individual in the group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{NP}$$\end{document}HNP or a non-human target L, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P_b^{H_{NP}}(p,q):=\frac{(1-p)H}{c(p,q)H+L}>P_b^{H_{NP}}(p,1),\quad \text {for}\quad p\in (0,1),\ q\in [0,1), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PbNP(p,q):=(1-p)Hc(p,q)H+L>PbNP(p,1),forp∈(0,1),q∈[0,1),and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{L}{c(p,q)H+L}> \frac{L}{H+L},\quad \text {for}\quad p\in (0,1),\ q\in [0,1), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Lc(p,q)H+L>LH+L,forp∈(0,1),q∈[0,1),respectively. Then, taking human protective behaviour into account, the model (1) becomes4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{array}{rl} S_{P}' & = -\beta _{H\leftarrow M}I_M \frac{q S_{P} }{c(p,q)H+L},\\ I_{P}' & = \beta _{H\leftarrow M}I_M \frac{q S_{P} }{c(p,q)H+L} - \gamma I_{P} ,\\ S_{NP}' & = -\beta _{H\leftarrow M}I_M \frac{S_{NP} }{c(p,q)H+L},\\ I_{NP}' & = \beta _{H\leftarrow M}I_M \frac{S_{NP} }{c(p,q)H+L} - \gamma I_{NP},\\ I_M' & = \beta _{M\leftarrow H}(M-I_M) \frac{q I_{P} +I_{NP} }{c(p,q)H+L} -\mu I_M, \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}SP′=-βH←MIMqSPc(p,q)H+L,IP′=βH←MIMqSPc(p,q)H+L-γIP,SNP′=-βH←MIMSNPc(p,q)H+L,INP′=βH←MIMSNPc(p,q)H+L-γINP,IM′=βM←H(M-IM)qIP+INPc(p,q)H+L-μIM,where now \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_P, I_P$$\end{document}SP,IP denote the susceptible and infectious individuals who adopt protective behaviour, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{NP}, I_{NP}$$\end{document}SNP,INP denote the susceptible and infectious individuals who do not. Moreover,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \beta _{H\leftarrow M}:=b p_{H\leftarrow M}, \qquad \beta _{M\leftarrow H}:=b p_{M\leftarrow H} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}βH←M:=bpH←M,βM←H:=bpM←Hare the transmission rates from mosquitoes to humans and from humans to mosquitoes, respectively.

Remark 2

In system (4), we assume that protective behaviour does not substantially alter the mosquito handling time; that is, the time required for a mosquito to attempt or complete a bite is assumed to be the same for protected and non-protected individuals. Consequently, protection affects only the probability of successful biting (through the factor q), and not the biting rate itself.

Remark 3

Consider the case L=0L=0. We note that neither of the limits\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{(p, q)\rightarrow (1^-, 0^+)}P_b^{H_P}(p, q) \;\;\hbox { and } \;\;\lim _{(p, q)\rightarrow (1^-, 0^+)}P_b^{H_{NP}}(p, q) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}lim(p,q)→(1-,0+)PbP(p,q)andlim(p,q)→(1-,0+)PbNP(p,q)exists. This “inconsistency” in the model is expected, as we are assuming that the number of available hosts is zero while the mosquito biting rate remains fixed; that is, mosquitoes are assumed to bite a fixed number of hosts per unit time regardless of host availability. Clearly, this situation cannot occur when L>0L>0.

In the following, we argue in terms of the fractions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_P/H$$\end{document}SP/H, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_P/H$$\end{document}IP/H, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{NP}/H$$\end{document}SNP/H, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{NP}/H$$\end{document}INP/H, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_M/M$$\end{document}IM/M, which, for later convenience, will still be denoted by the same variables that have been used so far. The resulting model reads5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{array}{rl} S_{P}' & = -\beta _{H\leftarrow M}\rho I_M \frac{q S_{P} }{c(p,q)+l},\\ I_{P}' & = \beta _{H\leftarrow M}\rho I_M \frac{q S_{P} }{c(p,q)+l} - \gamma I_{P} ,\\ S_{NP}' & = -\beta _{H\leftarrow M}\rho I_M \frac{S_{NP} }{c(p,q)+l},\\ I_{NP}' & = \beta _{H\leftarrow M}\rho I_M \frac{S_{NP} }{c(p,q)+l} - \gamma I_{NP},\\ I_M' & = \beta _{M\leftarrow H}(1-I_M) \frac{q I_{P} +I_{NP} }{c(p,q)+l} -\mu I_M, \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}SP′=-βH←MρIMqSPc(p,q)+l,IP′=βH←MρIMqSPc(p,q)+l-γIP,SNP′=-βH←MρIMSNPc(p,q)+l,INP′=βH←MρIMSNPc(p,q)+l-γINP,IM′=βM←H(1-IM)qIP+INPc(p,q)+l-μIM,where now6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} l:=\frac{L}{H},\quad \text {and}\quad \rho :=\frac{M}{H}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}l:=LH,andρ:=MH.For the reader’s convenience, we report the model parameters and their interpretation in Table 1, while c(p, q) is given by (3). As we are dealing with fractions, the biologically meaningful state space for the solutions of model (5) is the set\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Omega :=\{(&S_P, I_P, S_{NP}, I_{NP}, I_M) \in \mathbb {R}^5_{\ge 0}\ :\\&0\le S_P, I_P, S_{NP}, I_{NP}, I_M;\ {S_P+I_P\le p;\ S_{NP}+I_{NP}\le 1-p;}\ I_M\le 1\}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ω:={(SP,IP,SNP,INP,IM)∈R≥05:0≤SP,IP,SNP,INP,IM;SP+IP≤p;SNP+INP≤1-p;IM≤1},for which the following classical result holds.

Proposition 1

For every initial condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{X}}(0):=(S_P(0), I_P(0), S_{NP}(0), I_{NP}(0), I_M(0)) \in \Omega $$\end{document}X(0):=(SP(0),IP(0),SNP(0),INP(0),IM(0))∈Ω, the system (5) admits a unique solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{X}}(t):=(S_P(t), I_P(t), S_{NP}(t), I_{NP}(t), I_M(t)) \in \Omega $$\end{document}X(t):=(SP(t),IP(t),SNP(t),INP(t),IM(t))∈Ω that is globally defined in the future. Furthermore, the following inequalities hold for every t≥ 0t≥0:7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S_P(t)+I_P(t)\le S_P(0)+I_P(0),\quad \text {and}\quad S_{NP}(t)+I_{NP}(t)\le S_{NP}(0)+I_{NP}(0). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}SP(t)+IP(t)≤SP(0)+IP(0),andSNP(t)+INP(t)≤SNP(0)+INP(0).

Proof

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{X}}(0)\in \Omega $$\end{document}X(0)∈Ω. The local existence and uniqueness of the solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{X}}(t)$$\end{document}X(t) to the Cauchy problem associated with (5) follow from the Cauchy–Lipschitz theorem. Now, for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y\in \{ S_P, I_P, S_{NP}, I_{NP}, I_M\}$$\end{document}Y∈{SP,IP,SNP,INP,IM}, one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y'|_{Y=0}\ge 0$$\end{document}Y′|Y=0≥0, which implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{X}}(t)\in \mathbb {R}^5_{\ge 0}$$\end{document}X(t)∈R≥05 for all t≥ 0t≥0 for which the solution is defined. Furthermore, the inequalities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(S_j+I_j)'=-\gamma I_j\le 0,\quad j\in \{P,\ NP\}$$\end{document}(Sj+Ij)′=-γIj≤0,j∈{P,NP}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I'_M|_{I_M\ge 1} \le -\mu I_M\le 0$$\end{document}IM′|IM≥1≤-μIM≤0 hold. Hence, using standard arguments, one obtains that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{X}}(t)\in \Omega $$\end{document}X(t)∈Ω is globally defined in the future, and the inequalities in (7) hold. □ □

Finally, for later use, we introduce the removed fractions8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {R_P:=p-S_P-I_P,\quad \text {and}\quad R_{NP}:=(1-p) -S_{NP}-I_{NP},} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}RP:=p-SP-IP,andRNP:=(1-p)-SNP-INP,as well as the sums9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {S_H:=S_P+S_{NP},\quad I_H:=I_P+I_{NP},\quad \text {and}\quad R_H:=R_P+R_{NP}.} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}SH:=SP+SNP,IH:=IP+INP,andRH:=RP+RNP.

Control reproduction number

In epidemiological models, the BRN \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document}R0 is the expected number of secondary cases produced by a typical infected individual in a completely susceptible population during its entire infectious period (Diekmann et al. 1990). When intervention or control strategies are implemented in an otherwise fully susceptible population, the relevant threshold quantity is referred to as the Control Reproduction Number (CRN), denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_c$$\end{document}Rc. This quantity shares the same threshold property as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document}R0 (Pellis et al. 2022).

In this section, we compute the CRN \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_c:=R_c(p,q)$$\end{document}Rc:=Rc(p,q) for the model with protective behaviour (5), following the general framework introduced in Diekmann et al. (1990). In particular, we apply the Next Generation Matrix (NGM) method described in Diekmann et al. (2010), van den Driessche and Watmough (2002). To this end, we first observe that system (5) admits the Disease-Free Equilibrium (DFE)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (S_{P}^*,\ I_{P}^*,\ S_{NP}^*,\ I_{NP}^*,\ I_M^*):=(p,\ 0,\ 1-p,\ 0,\ 0). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(SP∗,IP∗,SNP∗,INP∗,IM∗):=(p,0,1-p,0,0).We then focus on the early stage of the epidemic. In particular, we assume that the total human susceptible fraction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_H$$\end{document}SH in (9) satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{H}\approx 1$$\end{document}SH≈1 and that the mosquito population is almost entirely susceptible, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_M=1-I_M\approx 1$$\end{document}SM=1-IM≈1. We therefore consider the linearised system for the infected compartments around the DFE10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{array}{rl} I_{P}' & = \beta _{H\leftarrow M}\rho I_M\frac{q p}{c(p,q)+l} - \gamma I_{P} ,\\ I_{NP}' & = \beta _{H\leftarrow M} \rho I_M\frac{1-p}{c(p,q)+l} - \gamma I_{NP} ,\\ I_M' & = \beta _{M\leftarrow H}\frac{q I_{P} +I_{NP} }{c(p,q)+l} -\mu I_M, \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}IP′=βH←MρIMqpc(p,q)+l-γIP,INP′=βH←MρIM1-pc(p,q)+l-γINP,IM′=βM←HqIP+INPc(p,q)+l-μIM,where ρ ρ and l are defined as in (6). Following Diekmann et al. (2010), van den Driessche and Watmough (2002), we define a matrix accounting for infection11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} B(p, q):=\begin{pmatrix} 0 & 0 & \dfrac{\rho qp\beta _{H\leftarrow M }}{c(p,q)+l}\\ 0 & 0 & \dfrac{\rho (1- p)\beta _{H\leftarrow M}}{c(p,q)+l}\\ \dfrac{q\beta _{M\leftarrow H}}{c(p,q)+l} & \dfrac{\beta _{M\leftarrow H}}{c(p,q)+l} & 0 \end{pmatrix},\quad p,q\in [0,1], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}B(p,q):=00ρqpβH←Mc(p,q)+l00ρ(1-p)βH←Mc(p,q)+lqβM←Hc(p,q)+lβM←Hc(p,q)+l0,p,q∈[0,1],and a matrix accounting for transition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma :={{\,\textrm{diag}\,}}(\gamma , \gamma , \mu )$$\end{document}Σ:=diag(γ,γ,μ), so that the Jacobian matrix of system (10) can be easily written as B(p,q)-Σ B(p,q)-Σ. Then, according to Diekmann et al. (1990), the CRN \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{c}(p, q)$$\end{document}Rc(p,q) is obtained as the spectral radius of the NGM \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K(p, q):=B(p, q)\Sigma ^{-1}$$\end{document}K(p,q):=B(p,q)Σ-1, and explicitly reads12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} R_c=R_0\; \frac{1+l}{c(p,q)+l}\;\sqrt{1-p(1-q^2)}\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Rc=R01+lc(p,q)+l1-p(1-q2),where13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} R_{0}:=\frac{1}{1+l}\; \sqrt{\frac{\beta _{H\leftarrow M}\beta _{M\leftarrow H}}{\gamma \mu } \rho } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}R0:=11+lβH←MβM←Hγμρis the BRN (namely, the reproduction number in the absence of protective behaviour).

Note that, if p=0p=0 (i.e. no individuals adopt protective behaviour) or q=1q=1 (i.e. protection is completely ineffective), then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_c=R_0$$\end{document}Rc=R0. Moreover, it is interesting to observe that if p=1p=1 and l=0l=0, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_c=R_0$$\end{document}Rc=R0. This is a direct consequence of the Ross–Macdonald-like assumptions underlying the model. Indeed, in this case, all individuals adopt protective behaviour. However, the mosquito biting rate is assumed to be fixed, meaning that mosquitoes feed on a given number of hosts per unit time regardless of host behaviour. Since l=0l=0, mosquitoes have no alternative hosts and therefore must feed on humans. Consequently, whenever protection fails, all mosquito bites are effectively concentrated on those individuals for whom protection is unsuccessful. As a result, the overall transmission potential remains unchanged.

It is therefore natural to ask whether the presence of awareness in the human population reduces the reproduction number. In particular, we seek conditions ensuring that14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} R_c(p, q)< R_{0}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Rc(p,q)<R0.In this regard, we have the following result.

Proposition 2

Let p∈ (0, 1)p∈(0,1), q∈ [0, 1)q∈[0,1) and l≥ 0l≥0. Then condition (14) holds if and only if15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} l>F(p,q),\qquad \text {with}\qquad F(p,q):=\frac{(1-p)(1-q)}{\sqrt{1-p(1-q^2)}+q}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}l>F(p,q),withF(p,q):=(1-p)(1-q)1-p(1-q2)+q.

Proof

From (12), the inequality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_c(p, q)< R_{0}$$\end{document}Rc(p,q)<R0 is equivalent to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{1+l}{(1-p+q p) + l}\sqrt{1-p+q^2 p}<1\iff (1+l)\sqrt{1-p+q^2 p}<(1-p+qp) + l\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}1+l(1-p+qp)+l1-p+q2p<1⟺(1+l)1-p+q2p<(1-p+qp)+l.Rearranging terms yields16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left[ \sqrt{1-p+q^2 p}-(1-p+q p)\right] +l\left[ \sqrt{1-p+q^2 p}-1\right] < 0\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}1-p+q2p-(1-p+qp)+l1-p+q2p-1<0.We consider the first term on the LHS of (16). Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{1-p+q^2 p}-(1-p+q p)= \sqrt{1-p(1-q^2)}-[1-p(1-q)]$$\end{document}1-p+q2p-(1-p+qp)=1-p(1-q2)-[1-p(1-q)] and, since 1-p(1-q)≥ 01-p(1-q)≥0 for all p, q∈ [0, 1]p,q∈[0,1], we get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{1-p(1-q^2)}-[1-p(1-q)]>0\iff (1-q)^2p(1-p)>0$$\end{document}1-p(1-q2)-[1-p(1-q)]>0⟺(1-q)2p(1-p)>0, which is obvious if q∈ [0, 1)q∈[0,1) and p∈ (0, 1)p∈(0,1). Hence\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sqrt{1-p+q^2 p}-(1-p+q p)> 0,\quad p\in (0,1),\quad q\in [0, 1). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}1-p+q2p-(1-p+qp)>0,p∈(0,1),q∈[0,1).As for the second term of inequality (16), it is clear that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{1-p+q^2 p} = \sqrt{1-p(1-q^2)}<1$$\end{document}1-p+q2p=1-p(1-q2)<1, for p∈ (0, 1]p∈(0,1] and q∈ [0, 1).q∈[0,1). Hence17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} l\left[ \sqrt{1-p+q^2 p}-1\right] < 0,\qquad p\in (0, 1],\quad q\in [0, 1). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}l1-p+q2p-1<0,p∈(0,1],q∈[0,1).This shows that the presence of alternative hosts l always reduces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_c$$\end{document}Rc when protective behaviour is present. Using (17), inequality (16) can be rewritten as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l[1-\sqrt{1-p+q^2 p}]> \sqrt{1-p+q^2 p}-(1-p+q p)$$\end{document}l[1-1-p+q2p]>1-p+q2p-(1-p+qp) which is equivalent to18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} l>\frac{\sqrt{1-p+q^2 p}-(1-p+q p)}{1-\sqrt{1-p+q^2 p}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}l>1-p+q2p-(1-p+qp)1-1-p+q2p.Finally, after algebraic manipulations, the right-hand side of (18) can be rewritten, for p,q∈ (0,1)p,q∈(0,1), as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\sqrt{1-p+q^2 p}-(1-p+q p)}{1-\sqrt{1-p+q^2 p}}&=\frac{(1-p)(1-q)}{\sqrt{1-p(1-q^2)}+q}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}1-p+q2p-(1-p+qp)1-1-p+q2p=(1-p)(1-q)1-p(1-q2)+q.□ □

Corollary 3

Let q∈ [0, 1)q∈[0,1) and l>0l>0. Then there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{p} < 1$$\end{document}p¯<1 such that (14) holds if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{p}< p < 1$$\end{document}p¯<p<1. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l \ge \bar{l}$$\end{document}l≥l¯, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{l}:=(1-q)/(1+q)$$\end{document}l¯:=(1-q)/(1+q) is the positive root of\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} l^2(1+q)+2ql-(1-q)=0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}l2(1+q)+2ql-(1-q)=0,then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{p} \le 0$$\end{document}p¯≤0, so that (14) holds for all p ∈ (0,1)p∈(0,1). On the other hand, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0< l < \bar{l}$$\end{document}0<l<l¯, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{p} \in (0,1)$$\end{document}p¯∈(0,1).

Proof

Rewrite (15) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q(p):=Ap^2+Bp+C<0$$\end{document}Q(p):=Ap2+Bp+C<0, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A:=(1-q)^2$$\end{document}A:=(1-q)2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B:=(1-q)[l^2(1+q)+2ql-2(1-q)]$$\end{document}B:=(1-q)[l2(1+q)+2ql-2(1-q)], and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C:=(1-q)[1-q(1+2\,l)-l^2(1+q)]$$\end{document}C:=(1-q)[1-q(1+2l)-l2(1+q)]. It is easy to see that Q(1)=0Q(1)=0, hence one root of the quadratic equation is p=1p=1. Moreover, Q'(1) > 0Q′(1)>0 for l > 0l>0, which implies that the other root \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{p}$$\end{document}p¯ satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{p}<1$$\end{document}p¯<1, and therefore \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Ap^2+Bp+C<0$$\end{document}Ap2+Bp+C<0 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\bar{p},1)$$\end{document}(p¯,1). Finally, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{p} \le 0$$\end{document}p¯≤0 if and only if Q(0) = C ≤ 0Q(0)=C≤0, which holds precisely when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l \ge \bar{l}$$\end{document}l≥l¯. □ □

Fig. 2The behaviour of the function F(p, q) defined in (15)

The behaviour of the function F(p, q) defined in (15)

The behaviour of the function F(p, q) is illustrated in Fig. 2. Note that it depends only on p and q, and is therefore independent of all other model parameters. Essentially, Proposition 2 shows that if there are not enough “other available hosts”, or, under the other interpretation of L (recall Remark 1), if human hosts are not sufficiently distanced from each other, then protective behaviour adopted by only part of the population may actually lead to an increase in the reproduction number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document}R0.

From Proposition 2, we also immediately obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 4

Let l=0l=0. Then, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_c(p, q)\ge R_{0},\ \forall p\in [0,1),\ q\in (0,1].$$\end{document}Rc(p,q)≥R0,∀p∈[0,1),q∈(0,1]. In particular, defining \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r(p, q):=R_c(p, q)/R_0$$\end{document}r(p,q):=Rc(p,q)/R0, we have r(1, q)=1r(1,q)=1, r(p, q)>1r(p,q)>1, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _q r(p,q) < 0$$\end{document}∂qr(p,q)<0 for all q ∈ [0,1)q∈[0,1) and p∈ (0,1)p∈(0,1).

Thus, either in the absence of other hosts or under conditions of high population density, static protective behaviour in the human population always increases the reproduction number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document}R0 when it is only partially adopted (p<1p<1) or partially effective (q>0q>0).

This is consistent with the results of Dye and Hasibeder (1986); Hasibeder and Dye (1988) for vector-host models with heterogeneous host populations. In particular, Dye and Hasibeder (1986) show that, in the absence of non-competent hosts, static heterogeneity in the host population always leads to an increased value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document}R0. Hasibeder and Dye (1988) further discuss the effect of such heterogeneity on the final epidemic size. See also the more recent work by Bolzoni et al. (2015), where, building on the results of Dye and Hasibeder (1986), Hasibeder and Dye (1988), the authors investigate the role of heterogeneity on the invasion probability of a vector-borne disease in multi-host models.

Finally, it is worth observing from (12) that the presence of other hosts, i.e. l>0l>0, always decreases the value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document}R0 regardless of the level of awareness or intervention, and even in their absence (i.e. when q=1q=1 or p=0p=0). This phenomenon is commonly known (especially in the context of tick-borne infections) as the dilution effect (Rosà and Pugliese 2007). Finally, considering \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_c(p, q)$$\end{document}Rc(p,q) as a function of q, it is straightforward to derive the following result.

Proposition 5

Let l > 0l>0. Then, for every p∈ (0,1]p∈(0,1], the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_c(p, q)$$\end{document}Rc(p,q), viewed as a function of q, attains a minimum value, smaller than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{0}$$\end{document}R0, at q= (1-p)/(1-p+l).q=(1-p)/(1-p+l).

Proposition 5 shows that when protective behaviour is static, the largest reduction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document}R0 is achieved at an intermediate level of protection effectiveness, 1-q1-q. Figure 3 illustrates the behaviour of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_c$$\end{document}Rc as a function of q for different values of p and l.Fig. 3Behaviour of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_c$$\end{document}Rc for model (5) as a function of q for different values of p and l, with ρ =2ρ=2 and epidemiological parameters as in Table 2

Asymptotic dynamics and final size

In this section, we study the long-term dynamics of the solutions of system (5) and we numerically investigate the effect of static protective behaviour in the host population on the final epidemic size (Brauer 2017, 2019; Diekmann et al. 2013). We begin by observing that, since the model does not include human demography or loss of immunity, the only non-trivial equilibria of (5) are those satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_P=I_{NP}=I_M=0$$\end{document}IP=INP=IM=0. Consequently, after an outbreak, the disease is expected to die out in the long run. This result is established in the following proposition.

Proposition 6

For system (5), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim _{t\rightarrow +\infty } I_{P}(t) = \lim _{t\rightarrow +\infty } I_{NP}(t) =\lim _{t\rightarrow +\infty } I_M(t)=0.$$\end{document}limt→+∞IP(t)=limt→+∞INP(t)=limt→+∞IM(t)=0.

Proof

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{P},\, S_{NP},\, I_{P},\, I_{NP}\, \in {\mathbb {R}}_{\ge 0}$$\end{document}SP,SNP,IP,INP∈R≥0, the inequalities in (7) hold. Hence, both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_P:=S_{P}+I_{P}$$\end{document}XP:=SP+IP and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_{NP}:=S_{NP}+I_{NP}$$\end{document}XNP:=SNP+INP are non-increasing functions of t, thus they admit limits \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_{P}^ \infty ,\, X_{NP}^\infty \ge 0$$\end{document}XP∞,XNP∞≥0, respectively, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}t→+∞. Then, we observe that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} -\infty < X_P^\infty -X_P(0) =-\gamma \int _0^{+\infty } I_{P}(t)\,\!\textrm{d}t, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-∞<XP∞-XP(0)=-γ∫0+∞IP(t)dt,which implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{P}(t)\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}IP(t)→0 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}t→+∞. The claim follows by applying the same reasoning to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{NP}$$\end{document}INP, and by noticing that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{P}(t),I_{NP}(t)\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}IP(t),INP(t)→0 implies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_M(t)\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}IM(t)→0. □ □

In the remainder of this section, we investigate how partial protective behaviour affects the final epidemic size. Note that, in general, deriving final size relations even for simple vector-borne epidemic models is not straightforward. Indeed, to the best of our knowledge, these aspects have only been investigated in a few recent papers by Brauer (2017, 2019) (see also Giménez-Mujica et al. (2023), Tsubouchi et al. (2019)), where the author derives approximate formulas that provide lower and upper bounds. See also Pugliese and De Reggi (2026) for further discussion.

In this section, building on the discussion above, we use numerical simulations to explore the effect of protective behaviour on the final epidemic size for model (5). In particular, by letting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_P$$\end{document}RP and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{NP}$$\end{document}RNP be defined as in (8), and recalling (9), we plot \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_P,\, Y_{NP}$$\end{document}YP,YNP, together with their sum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_H$$\end{document}YH, as well as the fractions within each subgroup, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_P/p$$\end{document}YP/p and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_{NP}/(1-p)$$\end{document}YNP/(1-p), for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y\in \{I, R\}$$\end{document}Y∈{I,R}. We then consider the (numerically approximated) limits\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^\infty _H:=\lim _{t\rightarrow +\infty } R_H(t),\quad \hat{R}^\infty _P:=\lim _{t\rightarrow +\infty } R_P(t)/p\quad \hbox { and }\quad \hat{R}^\infty _{NP}:=\lim _{t\rightarrow +\infty } R_{NP}(t)/(1-p),$$\end{document}RH∞:=limt→+∞RH(t),R^P∞:=limt→+∞RP(t)/pandR^NP∞:=limt→+∞RNP(t)/(1-p),which represent the total infected fraction (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^\infty _H$$\end{document}RH∞) and the infected fractions within each subgroup (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{R}^\infty _{P}$$\end{document}R^P∞ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{R}^\infty _{NP}$$\end{document}R^NP∞), respectively.Table 2Parameter values inspired by dengue fever and taken from Zhong et al.
