The top row of Fig. 4 shows a simulation for (5) with q = 0.2q=0.2 and p=0.6p=0.6, which yields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_c\approx 2.24>R_0$$\end{document}Rc≈2.24>R0. Observe that, although protective behaviour leads to an increased value of the reproduction number, the final epidemic size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^\infty _H$$\end{document}RH∞ is smaller than for the model without protective behaviour. This indicates that personal protection against mosquito bites can help reduce the total number of infections in the long run. Yet, the bottom row of Fig. 4 shows that, while protective behaviour decreases the proportion of individuals infected in the protected subpopulation (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{R}_P^\infty $$\end{document}R^P∞), the proportion infected in the non-protected subpopulation (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{R}_{NP}^\infty $$\end{document}R^NP∞) is larger than in the model without protection. A similar phenomenon was also observed in Hasibeder and Dye (1988) in the context of a vector-borne epidemic model with heterogeneity in mosquito preferences.

In contrast, in Fig. 5, where we take p=0.6p=0.6 but l=1l=1, protective behaviour not only effectively reduces the value of the reproduction number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document}R0, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_c\approx 1.82<R_0$$\end{document}Rc≈1.82<R0, but also decreases \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{R}_{NP}^\infty $$\end{document}R^NP∞. Moreover, the final epidemic size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_H^\infty $$\end{document}RH∞ is even slightly smaller than that observed in Fig. 4. A possible explanation lies in the larger value of l assumed here (recall that ρ ρ is adjusted accordingly to preserve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0\approx 2.12)$$\end{document}R0≈2.12). Indeed, although the value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document}R0 remains unchanged compared with the simulations in Fig. 4, the larger fraction of non-competent hosts (or, under the alternative interpretation of L, a greater spatial separation between human hosts) acts as a “shield”, mitigating the tendency of protective behaviour to concentrate mosquito bites in the non-protected group.

Information-dependent behavioural changes

In this section, we modify model (5) to allow individuals to dynamically adjust their behaviour based on the information currently available about an ongoing epidemic. To model this scenario, we follow the approach of d’Onofrio and Manfredi (2009, 2022), d’Onofrio et al. (2007) by introducing the information index J(t), which provides a summary of the publicly available information on the infection at time t≥ 0t≥0. In particular, we assume that J(t) depends on both the present and past prevalence (d’Onofrio and Manfredi 2009) of the vector-borne disease in the human population. More precisely, we define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J:[0, \infty )\rightarrow \mathbb {R}_{\ge 0}$$\end{document}J:[0,∞)→R≥0 as19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} J(t) :&=\int _{-\infty }^t (I_{P}(\theta ) + I_{NP}(\theta ))K(t-\theta )\,\!\textrm{d}\theta , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}J(t):=∫-∞t(IP(θ)+INP(θ))K(t-θ)dθ,where K is a non-negative measurable memory kernel satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _0^{\infty }K(\tau )\,\!\textrm{d}\tau = 1$$\end{document}∫0∞K(τ)dτ=1, which weights the contribution of current and past infections to the information available at present. We note that, in principle, J(t) may depend on a variety of (epidemiological) variables. For instance, it may depend on the current and past values of the human incidence, as in d’Onofrio and Manfredi (2022); see also Buonomo et al. (2025). Moreover, present and past prevalence values (which are assumed to be known precisely) are translated into information through the memory kernel K as in (19). It would be possible to assume that information depends on a non-linear increasing function of prevalence, or of incidence; however, for the sake of simplicity, we restrict our attention to equation (19).

We consider model (5) again. We assume that individuals can adjust their behaviour based on information about present and past prevalence, that is, on J(t). This implies that individuals may move between group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_P$$\end{document}HP and group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{NP}$$\end{document}HNP, according to the following system of ODEs:20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{array}{rl} H_{P}' & = a(J)H_{NP} - w(J) H_{P} ,\\ H_{NP}' & = -a(J)H_{NP} + w(J) H_{P}, \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HP′=a(J)HNP-w(J)HP,HNP′=-a(J)HNP+w(J)HP,which is obtained from the model proposed in Demers et al. (2018) by allowing the rates a and w to depend on J. We interpret variations in a (resp. w) as resulting from public health campaigns and/or other sources of information encouraging individuals to adopt (resp. ignore) protective measures against mosquito bites, as well as from the willingness of host individuals to comply (resp. not comply) with such recommendations. In particular, we assume that a and w satisfy the following requirements:

Assumption 1

a is a positive and increasing function, while w is a positive and non-increasing function; that is, a(x), w(x)>0a(x),w(x)>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a(0) \in \mathcal {O}(1)$$\end{document}a(0)∈O(1), a'(x)>0a′(x)>0 and w'(x)≤ 0w′(x)≤0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in [0, +\infty )$$\end{document}x∈[0,+∞).

Assumption 1 models a scenario in which public health campaigns and increasing information about the current epidemic have a beneficial effect on the host population, enhancing its awareness of the disease and, consequently, its willingness to adopt self-protective measures. Such assumptions are reasonable in the case of a first large outbreak of an infectious disease for which the host population has no prior experience with past epidemics, as in the case of vector-borne diseases in temperate European countries such as Italy.Fig. 6Flow chart for model (20)

Flow chart for model (20)

Figure 6 provides a schematic representation of the flow of (20). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p(t) :=H_P(t)/H$$\end{document}p(t):=HP(t)/H denote the fraction of individuals in the population adopting protective behaviour at time t (so that the fraction of those not adopting it is 1 - p1-p at all times). Using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H_P + H_{NP})' \equiv 0$$\end{document}(HP+HNP)′≡0, it follows that (20) can be conveniently rewritten as21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p'=a(J)(1-p)-w(J)p =a(J)-[a(J)+w(J)]p. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}p′=a(J)(1-p)-w(J)p=a(J)-[a(J)+w(J)]p.Then, taking into account (21), we modify system (5) as follows:22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{array}{rl} S_{P}' & = -\beta _{H\leftarrow M} \rho I_M \frac{q S_{P} }{c(p,q)+l} + a(J)S_{NP} - w(J) S_{P} ,\\ S_{NP}' & = -\beta _{H\leftarrow M} \rho I_M \frac{S_{NP} }{c(p,q)+l}-a(J)S_{NP} + w(J) S_{P} ,\\ I_{P}' & = \beta _{H\leftarrow M} \rho I_M \frac{q S_{P} }{c(p,q)+l} - \gamma I_{P} + a(J)I_{NP} - w(J) I_{P} ,\\ I_{NP}' & = \beta _{H\leftarrow M} \rho I_M \frac{S_{NP} }{c(p,q)+l} - \gamma I_{NP} - a(J)I_{NP} + w(J) I_{P} ,\\ p' & = a(J)-\left[ a(J)+w(J)\right] p,\\ I_M' & = \beta _{M\leftarrow H}(1-I_M) \frac{q I_{P} +I_{NP} }{c(p,q)+l} -\mu I_M ,\\ J(t) & =\displaystyle \int _{-\infty }^t (I_{P}(\theta ) + I_{NP}(\theta ))K(t-\theta )\,\!\textrm{d}\theta . \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}SP′=-βH←MρIMqSPc(p,q)+l+a(J)SNP-w(J)SP,SNP′=-βH←MρIMSNPc(p,q)+l-a(J)SNP+w(J)SP,IP′=βH←MρIMqSPc(p,q)+l-γIP+a(J)INP-w(J)IP,INP′=βH←MρIMSNPc(p,q)+l-γINP-a(J)INP+w(J)IP,p′=a(J)-a(J)+w(J)p,IM′=βM←H(1-IM)qIP+INPc(p,q)+l-μIM,J(t)=∫-∞t(IP(θ)+INP(θ))K(t-θ)dθ.where c(p,q)=1-p(1-q)c(p,q)=1-p(1-q), recalling (3).

Remark 4

A model similar to (22) was proposed in Roosa and Fefferman (2022), where the authors considered a much more complex system (including, among other features, human demography, stages of the mosquito life cycle, and additional interventions targeting the mosquito population) and allowed only susceptible individuals to change their behaviour with rates dependent on the current prevalence (i.e. in our notation, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J:=I_H$$\end{document}J:=IH). In contrast, protective behaviour in the infected and removed classes was assumed to be static. The model proposed here extends that of Roosa and Fefferman (2022) by allowing all human individuals to adjust their behaviour according to possibly delayed information on the prevalence.

In the following, for the sake of simplicity, we assume, as is common in the literature (d’Onofrio and Manfredi 2022; d’Onofrio et al. 2007), that the memory kernel is given by23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} K(\theta )=\frac{k^{n}\theta ^{n-1}e^{-k \theta }}{(n-1)!},\qquad \theta \in \mathbb {R}_{\ge 0},\quad n\in \mathbb {N},\quad k\in (0,+\infty ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}K(θ)=knθn-1e-kθ(n-1)!,θ∈R≥0,n∈N,k∈(0,+∞),that is, K is the density function of an Erlang distribution with shape parameter n, mean φ = n/kφ=n/k (where k is the rate parameter), and standard deviation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma = \sqrt{n}/k$$\end{document}σ=n/k. This “simplifying” assumption on K allows us to use the linear chain trick (Macdonald 1978; MacDonald 1989) to reduce the equation for J to a system of ODEs (Diekmann et al. 2018). In particular, for n=1n=1, one can rewrite the equation for J as24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} J' =k\left( I_{P} + I_{NP} \right) -k J, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}J′=kIP+INP-kJ,while for n≥ 1n≥1, it can be reduced to the following system of ODEs:25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{array}{rl} Z_1' & =k\left( I_{P} + I_{NP} \right) -k Z_1, \\ Z_i' & =kZ_{i-1} -kZ_i ,\qquad \qquad \qquad i=2,\dots , n, \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Z1′=kIP+INP-kZ1,Zi′=kZi-1-kZi,i=2,⋯,n,with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_n =J$$\end{document}Zn=J.

In the following, for the sake of generality, we avoid using (24) and refer only to (25), with the convention that, for n=1n=1, system (25) implicitly coincides with (24) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_1=Z_{n-1}=J$$\end{document}Z1=Zn-1=J. Moreover, we refer to φ φ as the “delay”, as it qualitatively represents the lag between the current time and the peak of the memory kernel. Note that, as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}k→+∞ and n is bounded, one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi , \sigma \rightarrow 0^+$$\end{document}φ,σ→0+; that is, K converges in distribution to a Dirac delta concentrated at 0. In this case, the information becomes instantaneous. On the other hand, if both n and k increase in such a way that φ φ remains constant, then the Erlang distribution becomes increasingly concentrated around its mean φ φ. In particular, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n,\, k\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}n,k→+∞ with n/k=φ n/k=φ, then the n-Erlang distribution approaches a discrete delay at a linear rate (Andò et al. 2020), i.e. it converges to a Dirac delta concentrated at φ φ. In this case, the model reduces to one with memory concentrated at t-φ t-φ.

Since we are dealing with fractions, the biologically meaningful state space for the solutions of model (22) turns out to be the set\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\begin{matrix} \Omega :=\{ (& S_P, S_{NP}, I_P, I_{NP}, p, I_M, Z_1, \dots , Z_{n-1}, J) \in \mathbb {R}^{6+n}_{\ge 0}\ :\\ & 0\le S_P, S_{NP}, I_P, I_{NP}, p, I_M, Z_i, J\le 1,\quad i=1,\dots , n-1,\\ & {S_P+I_P\le p,\ S_{NP}+I_{NP}\le 1-p}\}. \end{matrix}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ω:={(SP,SNP,IP,INP,p,IM,Z1,⋯,Zn-1,J)∈R≥06+n:0≤SP,SNP,IP,INP,p,IM,Zi,J≤1,i=1,⋯,n-1,SP+IP≤p,SNP+INP≤1-p}.The following result holds.

Proposition 7

For every initial condition\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}X(0):=(S_P(0), S_{NP}(0), I_P(0), I_{NP}(0), p(0), I_M(0), Z_1(0),\dots , Z_{n-1}(0), J(0)) \in \Omega ,\end{aligned}$$\end{document}X(0):=(SP(0),SNP(0),IP(0),INP(0),p(0),IM(0),Z1(0),⋯,Zn-1(0),J(0))∈Ω,system (22) admits a unique solution\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X(t):=(S_P(t), S_{NP}(t), I_P(t), I_{NP}(t), p(t), I_M(t), Z_1(t),\dots , Z_{n-1}(t), J(t)) \in \Omega ,$$\end{document}X(t):=(SP(t),SNP(t),IP(t),INP(t),p(t),IM(t),Z1(t),⋯,Zn-1(t),J(t))∈Ω,which is globally defined in the future. Furthermore, the inequality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_H(t)+I_H(t)\le S_H(0)+I_H(0)$$\end{document}SH(t)+IH(t)≤SH(0)+IH(0) holds for every t≥ 0t≥0.

Proof

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X(0)\in \Omega $$\end{document}X(0)∈Ω. The local existence and uniqueness of the solution X(t) of the Cauchy problem relevant to (22) follows from the Cauchy–Lipschitz theorem. Now, for each\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y\in \{ S_P, S_{NP}, I_P, I_{NP}, p, I_M, Z_1, \dots , Z_{n-1}, J\},$$\end{document}Y∈{SP,SNP,IP,INP,p,IM,Z1,⋯,Zn-1,J},one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y'|_{Y=0}\ge 0$$\end{document}Y′|Y=0≥0, which implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X(t)\in \mathbb {R}^{6+n}_{\ge 0}$$\end{document}X(t)∈R≥06+n for all t≥ 0t≥0 for which the solution is defined. Furthermore, the inequalities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(S_H+I_H)'=-\gamma I_H\le 0$$\end{document}(SH+IH)′=-γIH≤0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p'|_{p\ge 1}\le -\omega (J) p\le 0$$\end{document}p′|p≥1≤-ω(J)p≤0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I'_M|_{I_M\ge 1} \le -\mu I_M\le 0$$\end{document}IM′|IM≥1≤-μIM≤0 imply that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_P, S_{NP}, I_P, I_{NP}, p, I_M\le 1$$\end{document}SP,SNP,IP,INP,p,IM≤1. From this, one also gets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_P'|_{R_P=0}\ge 0$$\end{document}RP′|RP=0≥0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{NP}'|_{R_{NP}=0}\ge 0$$\end{document}RNP′|RNP=0≥0 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_P$$\end{document}RP and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_P+I_P\le p$$\end{document}SP+IP≤p and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_i'|_{Z_i\ge 1}\le 0$$\end{document}Zi′|Zi≥1≤0 for i=1,… , ni=1,⋯,n (recall \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} Hence, standard arguments give that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X(t)\in \Omega $$\end{document}X(t)∈Ω is globally defined in the future and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_H(t)+I_H(t)\le S_H(0)+I_H(0)$$\end{document}SH(t)+IH(t)≤SH(0)+IH(0) for all t≥ 0t≥0. □ □

Control reproduction number

Proposition 8

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_c$$\end{document}Rc be defined as in (12) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_0,\, w_0\in (0, +\infty )$$\end{document}a0,w0∈(0,+∞). Then30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{R}_c(p_0, q)<R_c(p_0, q),\quad \text {for all}\quad p_0\in (0, 1],\ q\in [0,1). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}R^c(p0,q)<Rc(p0,q),for allp0∈(0,1],q∈[0,1).Moreover, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_0,\, w_0\rightarrow 0^+$$\end{document}a0,w0→0+ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{a_0,\, 1)\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}lima0,w0→+∞R^c(p0,q)=R0c(p¯,q)(1+l)c(p¯,q)+l<R0,for allp¯∈(0,1],q∈[0,1).

Proof

Observe that33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Delta =1- p_0(1-q)-\frac{(1-q)(a_0 c(p_0,q)+\gamma p_0 q)}{a_0+w_0+\gamma }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Δ=1-p0(1-q)-(1-q)(a0c(p0,q)+γp0q)a0+w0+γ.Hence, relation (30) immediately follows from (29) and (33), since (33) is smaller than 1 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_0,\, w_0\in (0, +\infty )$$\end{document}a0,w0∈(0,+∞) and q∈ [0, 1)q∈[0,1). As for the limits in (31) and (32), it suffices to observe that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim \limits _{a_0,w_0\rightarrow 0^+}\frac{(1-q)(a_0 c(p_0,q)+\gamma p_0 q)}{a_0+w_0+\gamma }=(1-q)\underline{p}q, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}lima0,w0→0+(1-q)(a0c(p0,q)+γp0q)a0+w0+γ=(1-q)p_q,and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{a_0,w_0\rightarrow +\infty }\frac{p_0(1-q)^2w_0}{a_0+w_0+\gamma }=\lim _{a_0,w_0\rightarrow +\infty }\frac{(1-q)(a_0 c(p_0,q)+\gamma p_0 q)}{a_0+w_0+\gamma }=c(\bar{p},q)(1-q)\bar{p}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}lima0,w0→+∞p0(1-q)2w0a0+w0+γ=lima0,w0→+∞(1-q)(a0c(p0,q)+γp0q)a0+w0+γ=c(p¯,q)(1-q)p¯,which concludes the proof. □ □

As an application of Proposition 8, let us consider epidemiological parameters as in Table 2, with l=0.25l=0.25 and ρ =2ρ=2, p=0.6p=0.6 and q=0.2q=0.2 as in Fig. 4. Then formula (12) gives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_c\approx 2.24>2.12\approx R_0$$\end{document}Rc≈2.24>2.12≈R0. Let us take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_0=0.1,\ 1,\ 10,\ 100$$\end{document}w0=0.1,1,10,100 and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_0=w_0p/(1-p)$$\end{document}a0=w0p/(1-p), so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_0:=p$$\end{document}p0:=p. Then (29) gives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{R}_c\approx 2.00$$\end{document}R^c≈2.00 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_0=0.1$$\end{document}w0=0.1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{R}_c\approx 1.83$$\end{document}R^c≈1.83 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_0=1$$\end{document}w0=1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{R}_c\approx 1.79$$\end{document}R^c≈1.79 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_0=10,\ 100$$\end{document}w0=10,100, while the limit in (32) gives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{R}_c\approx 1.79$$\end{document}R^c≈1.79. Note that for all these choices of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_0$$\end{document}w0, one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{R}_c< R_0<R_c$$\end{document}R^c<R0<Rc.

Separation of time scales

In this section, we assume that the dynamics of model (22) evolves on two distinct time scales. In particular, as is common in the literature, we assume that behavioural changes occur on a faster time scale than the epidemiological dynamics. On the one hand, as observed in Demers et al. (2018), if the epidemiological dynamics evolves much faster than the behavioural ones, then model (22) effectively reduces, on the fast time scale, to the model with static protective behaviour in (5). In this case, the dynamics induced by protective behaviour is entirely determined by the initial fraction of individuals adopting protection, with no information-driven effects. On the other hand, it is more interesting, from both a biological and a mathematical perspective, to investigate the role of information-induced behavioural changes when these occur on a much faster time scale than the epidemiological dynamics. This assumption is also common in the literature; see, for instance, Bulai et al. (2024), Della Marca et al. (2024), Poletti et al. (2009). Note that the distribution of the information memory J itself introduces an intrinsic temporal scale related to the possibly delayed acquisition of information about the epidemic from either the Public Health System (PHS) or the population. This may be due to the time lags in testing, reporting new cases and/or hospitalisations. Yet, such time scale does not necessarily coincide with that of opinion dynamics, as individuals may quickly react to new information once this is provided. We also note that in Demers et al. (2018), the authors heuristically discussed the case of infinitely large (and information-independent) switching rates, relating a two-group model with dynamical behavioural changes to a homogeneous-population model. In contrast to the previous discussion, in this section, we provide a rigorous derivation of this reduction to a homogeneous population model using an approach based on GSPT.

To this aim, we assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _{H\leftarrow M},\,\beta _{M\leftarrow H},\,\gamma ,\,\mu ,\,{k} \in \mathcal {O}(\varepsilon )$$\end{document}βH←M,βM←H,γ,μ,k∈O(ε), for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\varepsilon \ll 1$$\end{document}0<ε≪1, while all remaining parameters are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(1)$$\end{document}O(1). This qualitatively corresponds to assuming that behavioural adjustment occurs much faster than both the epidemic dynamics and the evolution of the information index, i.e. the system evolves on two distinct time scales. Under these assumptions, with a slight abuse of notation to avoid the introduction of four new parameters, model (22) may be rewritten as34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{array}{rl} S_{P}' & = -\varepsilon \beta _{H\leftarrow M}\rho I_M \frac{q S_{P} }{c(p,q)+l} + a(J)S_{NP} - w(J) S_{P} ,\\ S_{NP}' & = -\varepsilon \beta _{H\leftarrow M}\rho I_M \frac{S_{NP} }{c(p,q)+l}-a(J)S_{NP} + w(J) S_{P} ,\\ I_{P}' & = \varepsilon \beta _{H\leftarrow M} \rho I_M \frac{q S_{P} }{c(p,q)+l} - \varepsilon \gamma I_{P} + a(J)I_{NP} - w(J) I_{P} ,\\ I_{NP}' & = \varepsilon \beta _{H\leftarrow M} \rho I_M \frac{S_{NP} }{c(p,q)+l} - \varepsilon \gamma I_{NP} - a(J)I_{NP} + w(J) I_{P} ,\\ p' & = a(J)-\left[ a(J)+w(J)\right] p,\\ I_M' & = \varepsilon \beta _{M\leftarrow H}(1-I_M) \frac{q I_{P} +I_{NP} }{c(p,q)h+l} -\varepsilon \mu I_M ,\\ Z_1' & =\varepsilon k\left( I_{P} + I_{NP} \right) -\varepsilon k Z_1 \\ Z_i' & =\varepsilon kZ_{i-1} -\varepsilon kZ_i , \qquad i=2,\dots , n, \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}SP′=-εβH←MρIMqSPc(p,q)+l+a(J)SNP-w(J)SP,SNP′=-εβH←MρIMSNPc(p,q)+l-a(J)SNP+w(J)SP,IP′=εβH←MρIMqSPc(p,q)+l-εγIP+a(J)INP-w(J)IP,INP′=εβH←MρIMSNPc(p,q)+l-εγINP-a(J)INP+w(J)IP,p′=a(J)-a(J)+w(J)p,IM′=εβM←H(1-IM)qIP+INPc(p,q)h+l-εμIM,Z1′=εkIP+INP-εkZ1Zi′=εkZi-1-εkZi,i=2,⋯,n,with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_n =J$$\end{document}Zn=J. Letting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \rightarrow 0^+$$\end{document}ε→0+ in system (34), we obtain the corresponding so-called layer system35\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{array}{rl} {S_{P}'} & {= a(J)S_{NP} - w(J) S_{P} ,}\\ {S_{NP}' }& {= -a(J)S_{NP} + w(J) S_{P},}\\ {I_{P}'} & {= a(J)I_{NP} - w(J) I_{P} ,}\\ {I_{NP}' }& {= -a(J)I_{NP} + w(J) I_{P},}\\ p' & = a(J)-\left[ a(J)+w(J)\right] p,\\ J'& =0. \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}SP′=a(J)SNP-w(J)SP,SNP′=-a(J)SNP+w(J)SP,IP′=a(J)INP-w(J)IP,INP′=-a(J)INP+w(J)IP,p′=a(J)-a(J)+w(J)p,J′=0.Note that we omit the equations for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_M$$\end{document}IM and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_1,\dots , Z_{n-1}$$\end{document}Z1,⋯,Zn-1, since they neither evolve on this time scale, nor appear in the remaining equations. Proposition 9 ensures that the dynamics of system (34) is attracted to the critical manifold (37), where the small parameters begin to play a major role. Remark 5

We have h(p(x), q)>0h(p(x),q)>0 for q∈ [0, 1)q∈[0,1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in [0, +\infty )$$\end{document}x∈[0,+∞). Thus, equation (40) ensures that h(p(x), q) admits limit for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}x→+∞, and the largest reduction for the transmission rates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _{H\leftarrow M},\,\beta _{M\leftarrow H}$$\end{document}βH←M,βM←H is obtained at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{h}:=\lim _{x\rightarrow +\infty } h(p(x), q)$$\end{document}h¯:=limx→+∞h(p(x),q).

Lastly, we establish the following result on the asymptotic behaviour of the solutions of system (38), which follows directly by arguing as in the proof of Proposition 6 and using Proposition 7.

Proposition 10

For system (38), one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim _{\tau \rightarrow +\infty } I_H(\tau ) =\lim _{\tau \rightarrow +\infty } I_M(\tau ) =0$$\end{document}limτ→+∞IH(τ)=limτ→+∞IM(τ)=0.

Consequently, as expected, the dynamics of (38) always ends with epidemic extinction, independently of the hosts’ protective behaviour. However, we are interested in the transient behaviour of the solutions of system (38), which is far from trivial due to the absence of non-trivial equilibria (i.e. with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_H\ne 0$$\end{document}IH≠0 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_M\ne 0$$\end{document}IM≠0).

Numerical simulations

In this section, we present numerical experiments for the proposed model with behavioural changes, comparing the outputs of model (22) with those of the reduced one-group model (38). The purpose of the following simulations is to assess which dynamical features of the two-group model are reproduced by the reduced one-group model as the behavioural switching rates increase. In particular, we compare epidemic peak size and timing, the overall infected fraction, and convergence to the critical manifold. For the simulations, we use epidemiological parameters appropriate for a dengue outbreak, listed in Table 2. In addition, we consider l=0.25l=0.25 and l=1l=1, and we vary ρ ρ to preserve the value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0\approx 2.12$$\end{document}R0≈2.12, recalling (13). More precisely, we take ρ =2ρ=2 for l=0.25l=0.25 and ρ = 5.12ρ=5.12 for l=1l=1. We assume that, at the beginning of the outbreak, the human population is at the opinion equilibrium, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p(0)=p_0$$\end{document}p(0)=p0, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_0$$\end{document}p0 defined as in (27). As for the rates a and w, we consider the following forms:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a(x)=a_0+\alpha x,\quad a_0,\ \alpha> 0,\quad \text {and}\quad w(x)\equiv w_0,\quad w_0>0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}a(x)=a0+αx,a0,α>0,andw(x)≡w0,w0>0.We then vary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_0>0$$\end{document}w0>0, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_0=w_0$$\end{document}a0=w0, so that, according to (27), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_0=0.5$$\end{document}p0=0.5, i.e. at the beginning of the epidemic, 50%50% of the host population adopts personal protective measures against mosquito bites, possibly an optimistic assumption. We set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha = \chi \, w_0$$\end{document}α=χw0, where χ >0χ>0 accounts for both the effect of PHS campaigns and the human reaction to information. Finally, we vary q∈ [0, 1)q∈[0,1), the shape parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N, and the delay φ φ of the Erlang distribution in (23). Since we observe the outbreak from its very beginning, we consider the initial conditions\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\begin{gathered} S_P(0)=p_0,\quad S_{NP}(0)=1-S_P(0),\quad I_P(0)=I_{NP}(0)=0,\quad I_M(0)=10^{-4}. \end{gathered}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}SP(0)=p0,SNP(0)=1-SP(0),IP(0)=INP(0)=0,IM(0)=10-4.The numerical results are obtained using the MATLAB built-in ODE solvers ode45 and ode23s with standard tolerances (the latter is employed when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_0\ge 10$$\end{document}w0≥10).

Remark 6

The term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_0$$\end{document}w0 in α α is used to normalise the human response to information with respect to the magnitude of the reaction term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_0$$\end{document}w0. To clarify the choice of α α, let us consider the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=I_H$$\end{document}J=IH, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_H$$\end{document}IH is defined as in (9). In this case, taking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi =10^4$$\end{document}χ=104 yields, for the one-group model (36), that a(x)=2w(x)a(x)=2w(x) exactly when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x=J=I_H=10^{-4}$$\end{document}x=J=IH=10-4, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_0=w_0$$\end{document}a0=w0. Hence, for instance, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H=10^5$$\end{document}H=105, then a(x)=2w(x)a(x)=2w(x) when the total number of infected humans at t≥ 0t≥0 is equal to 10. Moreover, for these choices of a and w, it is straightforward to verify that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} h(p(x), q)=\frac{1}{2}h(0, q)\iff x =\frac{a_0q(1+2l)+l(w_0-a_0)+w_0}{\alpha [l-q(1+2l)]},\quad l\ne q(1+2l). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}h(p(x),q)=12h(0,q)⟺x=a0q(1+2l)+l(w0-a0)+w0α[l-q(1+2l)],l≠q(1+2l).In particular, in the case of perfect protection, q=0q=0, if l=0.25l=0.25, the “contact rates” between mosquitoes and humans are halved exactly when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_H=4\cdot 10^{-4}$$\end{document}IH=4·10-4 (i.e. when the total number of infected individuals is 40), while for q=0.1q=0.1 (90%90% of efficacy), one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_H=1.2\cdot 10^{-3}$$\end{document}IH=1.2·10-3.

We start by comparing the outputs of (22) with those of (38) for different orders of magnitude of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_0$$\end{document}w0. In Fig. 7, we take l=0.25l=0.25 and ρ =2ρ=2, and we observe that, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_0=10$$\end{document}w0=10 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {days}^{-1}$$\end{document}days-1 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_0=100$$\end{document}w0=100 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {days}^{-1}$$\end{document}days-1 (i.e. it is from two to three orders of magnitude larger than γ γ), the results for the two models are (almost) indistinguishable. In particular, note that in the plots for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_0=10, 100$$\end{document}w0=10,100 in the right column, the line corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_P/S_H$$\end{document}SP/SH perfectly overlaps with those corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_P/I_H, p$$\end{document}IP/IH,p and a(J)/[a(J)+w(J)]a(J)/[a(J)+w(J)], as the solution of (2) almost immediately converges to the critical manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}_0$$\end{document}C0 in (37). Interestingly, even for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_0=1$$\end{document}w0=1, the simulation of system (38) provides a good approximation of model (22), although the convergence to the manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}_0$$\end{document}C0 is much slower in this case, leading to significantly different timing and magnitudes of the (first) epidemic peak.

Finally, we can observe that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_0=0.1$$\end{document}w0=0.1 (i.e. of the same order of magnitude as γ γ), the dynamics of the two models differ substantially, as the solution is still far from converging to the manifold (37). This is also the only case, among those considered here, for which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{R}_c\approx 2.17>2.12 \approx R_0 $$\end{document}R^c≈2.17>2.12≈R0; see the caption of Fig. 7. Hence, in this case, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_0$$\end{document}w0 is not large enough to prevent protective behaviour from increasing the probability of outbreak.

In Figs. 8, 9, and 10, we plot \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_H$$\end{document}IH (left and centre) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_H$$\end{document}RH (right), defined as in (9), as functions of time for n=1, 2, 5n=1,2,5, respectively, and for three different values of q, namely q=0q=0 (upper row), q=0.1q=0.1 (middle row), and q=0.2q=0.2 (lower row). We observe that for q=0q=0 and q=0.1q=0.1, information-induced behavioural changes effectively prevent a large portion of infections for all choices of n (note the different scales on the vertical axes for the case q=0q=0 and q=0.1q=0.1). Interestingly, in the first and second rows, after the first epidemic peak, the solutions for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_H$$\end{document}IH appear to enter a quasi-stationary state, although the model does not admit any non-trivial equilibria (i.e. with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_H, I_M\ne 0$$\end{document}IH,IM≠0). Notably, this phenomenon is entirely due to the behavioural adaptation in response to the epidemic and does not correspond to convergence of the solutions of (22) and (38) to true equilibria of their respective systems. Indeed, observe that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_H$$\end{document}RH increases in all cases, indicating that no stationary situation has been reached by the solution, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_H$$\end{document}IH will inevitably go extinct in the long run, as shown in Proposition 10. Furthermore, when the memory distribution is more concentrated around its mean (and hence further in the past), as in Figs. 9 and 10, we observe the possible emergence of multiple epidemic waves. Notably, this occurs precisely in the cases where behavioural changes effectively prevent a large proportion of infections, namely for q=0q=0 and q=0.1q=0.1. On the other hand, for q=0.2q=0.2, although the adoption of protective measures still contributes to containing the epidemic, a larger outbreak is observed compared to the cases q=0q=0 and q=0.1q=0.1, and no oscillatory behaviour or quasi-stationary regimes arise.Fig. 11Plots for (22) (red for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_0=1$$\end{document}w0=1, yellow for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_0=0.1$$\end{document}w0=0.1) and (38) (blue, one-group model) with model parameters and initial conditions as in Sect. 4.1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi =10^4$$\end{document}χ=104, q=0.5q=0.5, n=1n=1, and φ =30φ=30 days in (23). Finally, in Fig. 11 we compare the outputs of models (22) and (38) for l=0.25l=0.25 and l=1l=1 (hence with ρ =2ρ=2 ad ρ =5.12ρ=5.12, respectively) and q=0.5q=0.5 (i.e. a 50% probability of protection failure). In this case, we observe, in both the upper and lower rows, that the rates at which individuals change their behaviour in response to information do not significantly affect the overall dynamics. Nevertheless, both scenarios show that the model with protective behaviour leads to a smaller final size compared to the model without protection (q=1q=1). In particular, the model with l=1l=1 predicts a smaller final size than the model with l=0.25l=0.25.

Behaviour-induced dynamics in the low attack rate regime

In this section, motivated by the results of Sect. 4, we investigate the dynamics of model (38) in a scenario where interventions and behavioural changes have successfully prevented a large proportion of infections before herd immunity is achieved and seasonality becomes dominant. Observe that the resulting systems resemble the model studied in Zhang et al. (2023), and subsequently extended in Andò et al. In the following section, we will investigate the existence and stability of the equilibria of (41) to gain insights into the transient dynamics of models (22) and (38).

Equilibria

In this section, we investigate stationary solutions of system (41). Convergence to these equilibria corresponds to a rapid “collapsing” of the fast–slow system onto its critical manifold, in whose neighbourhood the slow dynamics play a central role. Characterising the existence of such equilibria and the associated convergence allows us to describe the transient behaviour of the system, even though the asymptotic dynamics inevitably leads to disease extinction, as shown in Proposition 10.

We observe that the equilibria of system (41) are all points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} Theorem 11

Proof

Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<p^*<1$$\end{document}0<p∗<1 by condition (47). Furthermore, from is strictly increasing, and Assumption 1 it follows that p(· )p(·) is strictly increasing, and hence (48) implies the existence of a unique \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_H^*$$\end{document}IH∗ such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p(I_H^*)=p^*$$\end{document}p(IH∗)=p∗. A straightforward computation, recalling the definition (39), shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p^*$$\end{document}p∗ satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(p^*,q)=1/\hat{R}_e$$\end{document}h(p∗,q)=1/R^e, so that (46) holds. Uniqueness then follows from the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p^*$$\end{document}p∗ is the only solution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(p,q)=1/\hat{R}_e$$\end{document}h(p,q)=1/R^e, together with the injectivity of p(· )p(·). □ □

Remark 7

Since f(p):=h(p,q)f(p):=h(p,q) is a decreasing function, (48) is equivalent to51\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\lim _{x \rightarrow +\infty }h(p(x),q)< h(p^*,q)=\frac{1}{\hat{R}_e}< h(p(0),q)\nonumber \\&\quad \iff \hat{R}_e \lim _{x \rightarrow +\infty }h(p(x),q)< 1 < \hat{R}_e h(p(0),q). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limx→+∞h(p(x),q)<h(p∗,q)=1R^e<h(p(0),q)⟺R^elimx→+∞h(p(x),q)<1<R^eh(p(0),q).Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ q/(q+l)=h(1,q) \le \lim _{x \rightarrow +\infty }h(p(x),q) $$\end{document}q/(q+l)=h(1,q)≤limx→+∞h(p(x),q) and h(p(0),q) < h(0,q) = 1/(1+l)h(p(0),q)<h(0,q)=1/(1+l), it follows that (51) implies (47). Therefore, Theorem 11 can equivalently be restated by saying that a non-trivial equilibrium of (41) exists if and only if (51) holds.

Note that, while J(t) defined in (22) satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le J(t) \le 1$$\end{document}0≤J(t)≤1, as proved in Proposition 7, J(t) solution of (41) is a rescaling of the former, and we can only assume J(t) ≥ 0J(t)≥0; this explains why we consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim _{x \rightarrow +\infty }p(x)$$\end{document}limx→+∞p(x) instead of p(1) in (48) and (51).

Remark 8

In the following, in accordance with Andò et al. (2026), we refer to the non-trivial equilibrium in (50) as the “Established Equilibrium” (EsE) rather than the “Endemic Equilibrium”, as it represents a stationary situation reached by the system only due to behavioural changes during an outbreak, rather than in a truly endemic setting. However, we stress that, inevitably, the system (38) will asymptotically result in the extinction of the disease, see Proposition 10.

The two conditions of Theorem 11 show that the existence of the EsE depends, on the one hand, on the relation between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{R}_e$$\end{document}R^e, l, and q, on the other hand, on the function p(· )p(·), which determines how humans react to information on prevalence. In particular, the more effective the protection, the less restrictive the upper bound in (47) for the existence of an EsE. On the contrary, the larger l, the more restrictive the lower bound in (47). From this observation, for small l≠ 0l≠0 and large l/q, we can expect the outbreak to persist for a longer time. Note that this situation actually corresponds to a mosquito-borne epidemic in a region where high human density and low availability of alternative blood sources do not limit transmission. In particular, as some individuals adopt highly effective protective measures, mosquitoes concentrate their bites on unprotected individuals, thereby increasing the likelihood of a full transmission cycle (vector→ →human→ →vector).

Finally, we observe that, for l=0l=0, one has h≡ 1h≡1. Hence, we have the following:

Corollary 12

If l=0l=0, then system (41) admits the DFE only.

Stability of equilibria

This section aims to investigate the local stability of the equilibria of (41). We do this by linearising (41) around a given equilibrium \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\bar{I}_H,\bar{I}_M,\bar{J})$$\end{document}(I¯H,I¯M,J¯) and analysing the sign of the real parts of the eigenvalues of the corresponding Jacobian. First, we show that the DFE is Locally Asymptotically Stable (LAS) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{R}_e h(p(0), q)<1$$\end{document}R^eh(p(0),q)<1, while it is unstable when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{R}_e h(p(0), q)>1}$$\end{document}R^eh(p(0),q)>1, independently of the choice of K in (19) and (23). Then, we consider the stability of the EsE, showing that it depends on the specific form of the memory kernel. In particular, for selected choices of K, we show that the EsE may either be LAS or lose its stability via Hopf, hence ensuring the possible emergence of self-sustained oscillations even in an outbreak scenario.

Let52\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(x):=h(p(x), q),\qquad x\in [0,+\infty ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(x):=h(p(x),q),x∈[0,+∞),so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f'(x)= \partial _p h(p(x), q)\cdot p'(x)$$\end{document}f′(x)=∂ph(p(x),q)·p′(x). The linearisation of (41) around an equilibrium \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\bar{I}_H, \bar{I}_M, \bar{J})$$\end{document}(I¯H,I¯M,J¯) reads53\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{array}{rl} \dot{I}_H & = \beta _{H\leftarrow M}\rho S_Hf(\bar{J})I_M+\beta _{H\leftarrow M}\rho \bar{I}_M S_Hf'(\bar{J})J - \gamma I_H ,\\ \dot{I}_M & = \beta _{M\leftarrow H} f(\bar{J})I_H+\beta _{M\leftarrow H} f'(\bar{J}) \bar{I}_H J-\mu I_M,\\ \dot{Z}_1 & =k I_H -k Z_1 \\ \dot{Z}_i & = k Z_{i-1} - k Z_i ,\qquad \qquad \qquad i=2,\dots , n-1,\\ \dot{J} & =kZ_{n-1} -k J. \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}I˙H=βH←MρSHf(J¯)IM+βH←MρI¯MSHf′(J¯)J-γIH,I˙M=βM←Hf(J¯)IH+βM←Hf′(J¯)I¯HJ-μIM,Z˙1=kIH-kZ1Z˙i=kZi-1-kZi,i=2,⋯,n-1,J˙=kZn-1-kJ.Then, for K as in (23), the Jacobian associated with system (53) is the following:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ {\textbf{J}=\begin{pmatrix} -\gamma & \beta _{H\leftarrow M}\rho S_Hf(\bar{J}) & 0 & 0 & \dots & \beta _{H\leftarrow M}\rho \bar{I}_M S_Hf'(\bar{J})\\ \beta _{M\leftarrow H} f(\bar{J}) & -\mu & 0 & 0 & \dots & \beta _{M\leftarrow H} f'(\bar{J}) \bar{I}_H\\ k & 0 & -k & 0 & \dots & 0\\ 0 & 0 & k & -k & \dots & 0 \\ \dots & \dots & \dots & \dots & \dots & \dots \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \dots & k & -k \end{pmatrix}.} $$\end{document}J=-γβH←MρSHf(J¯)00⋯βH←MρI¯MSHf′(J¯)βM←Hf(J¯)-μ00⋯βM←Hf′(J¯)I¯Hk0-k0⋯000k-k⋯0⋯⋯⋯⋯⋯⋯000⋯k-k.By proceeding as in Sect.
