Remark 9

The derivation of (54) is provided in Sect. B, where we apply the relevant theory for delay equations (Diekmann et al. 1995) with, possibly, infinite delay (Diekmann and Gyllenberg 2012). As a result, equation (54) holds for any memory kernel K, not necessarily restricted to Erlang-distributed.

Stability of the DFE

Let us consider the DFE \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\bar{I}_H, \bar{I}_M, \bar{J})=(0, 0, 0)$$\end{document}(I¯H,I¯M,J¯)=(0,0,0). Then, the characteristic equation in (54) becomes56\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda ^2+\lambda (\gamma +\mu )+\gamma \mu \left[ 1 -\hat{R}_e^2f^2(0)\right] =0,\qquad \Re (\lambda )>-k. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λ2+λ(γ+μ)+γμ1-R^e2f2(0)=0,ℜ(λ)>-k.Recalling equation (52), we have the following result.

Theorem 13

The DFE is LAS when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{R}_eh(p(0), q)<1$$\end{document}R^eh(p(0),q)<1 and unstable when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{R}_eh(p(0), q)>1$$\end{document}R^eh(p(0),q)>1, independently of K (i.e. not restricted to the one in (23)). In particular, in the latter case, the characteristic equation (56) has exactly two real roots, one negative and one positive.

Proof

Being \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ,\ \mu >0$$\end{document}γ,μ>0, Descartes’ rule of signs ensures that the DFE is stable when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1-\hat{R}_eh(p(0), q)>0$$\end{document}1-R^eh(p(0),q)>0, while it is unstable when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1-\hat{R}_eh(p(0), q)<0$$\end{document}1-R^eh(p(0),q)<0. □ □

Comparing this condition with (51), we see that, when the DFE is stable, no EsE exists. Conversely, when the DFE is unstable, one additional condition is still needed to guarantee the existence of the EsE.

Stability of the EsE

Let us consider the EsE defined as in (50). Using (45) and (55), the characteristic equation (54) reads57\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda ^2+\lambda (\gamma +\mu )+\left( \frac{k}{\lambda +k}\right) ^n\gamma \delta \left( \lambda + 2\mu \right) =0, \qquad \Re (\lambda )>-k, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λ2+λ(γ+μ)+kλ+knγδλ+2μ=0,ℜ(λ)>-k,for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta :=-f'(I_H^*)\hat{R}_e I_H^*>0$$\end{document}δ:=-f′(IH∗)R^eIH∗>0. Note that, in this case, the stability of the equilibrium depends on the particular choice of n and k. In particular, from (57) we are led to study the roots of58\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda ^2(\lambda +k)^n+\lambda (\gamma +\mu )(\lambda +k)^n+ \lambda (k^n\gamma \delta )+2 k^n\gamma \delta \mu =0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λ2(λ+k)n+λ(γ+μ)(λ+k)n+λ(knγδ)+2knγδμ=0.It is worth observing that, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}k→+∞ and n is fixed, one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi :=n/k\rightarrow 0^+$$\end{document}φ:=n/k→0+ (that is, the information is instantaneous) and the characteristic equation reduces to59\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda ^2+\lambda \left( \gamma +\mu + \gamma \delta \right) + 2\mu \gamma \delta =0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λ2+λγ+μ+γδ+2μγδ=0.This case corresponds to the case in which K is a Dirac delta concentrated at 0, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{K}\equiv 1$$\end{document}K^≡1. On the other hand, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n,\ k\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}n,k→+∞ in such a way that φ :=n/kφ:=n/k is constant, then the kernel concentrates at t-φ t-φ, i.e. the memory becomes discrete with delay φ φ. In this case, the characteristic equation takes the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda ^2+\lambda (\gamma +\mu )+ \gamma \delta \lambda {\mathop {\textrm{e}}\limits }^{-\lambda \varphi }+2 \gamma \mu \delta e^{-\lambda \varphi }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λ2+λ(γ+μ)+γδλe-λφ+2γμδe-λφ.We consider the following cases: instantaneous information, exponentially fading memory, and Erlang-2 distributed memory.

Instantaneous information : the characteristic equation reads as in equation (59). Then, from (40), the Routh-Hurwitz criterion implies that the EsE is LAS whenever it exists.

Exponentially fading memory : we assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K(\theta )=k{\mathop {\textrm{e}}\limits }^{-k\theta }$$\end{document}K(θ)=ke-kθ, corresponding to n=1n=1 in (23). Then, equation (58) reduces to60\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda ^2(\lambda +k)+\lambda (\gamma +\mu )(\lambda +k)+ k\gamma \delta \left( \lambda + 2\mu \right) =0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λ2(λ+k)+λ(γ+μ)(λ+k)+kγδλ+2μ=0.Applying the Routh–Hurwitz criterion for third-order polynomials (see Sect. C for details), we obtain the following result.

Proposition 14

Let the assumptions of Theorem 11 hold, and let n=1n=1 in (23). Then the EsE is LAS if and only if61\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} k>-\frac{\gamma \delta (\gamma -\mu )+(\gamma +\mu )^2}{\gamma +\mu +\gamma \delta }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}k>-γδ(γ-μ)+(γ+μ)2γ+μ+γδ.If equality holds, the EsE undergoes a Hopf bifurcation, and the characteristic equation (60) has one negative real root and a pair of purely imaginary conjugate roots. If the opposite inequality holds, then the EsE is unstable, and the characteristic equation (60) has one negative real root and a pair of complex conjugate roots with positive real parts.

Since condition (40) holds and k>0k>0, condition (61) is automatically satisfied whenever \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma +k\ge \mu $$\end{document}γ+k≥μ. In particular, this is always true if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \ge \mu $$\end{document}γ≥μ. This assumption is biologically reasonable, as the average human infectious period 1/γ 1/γ is typically shorter than the average mosquito lifespan 1/μ 1/μ. On the other hand, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma <\mu $$\end{document}γ<μ, then condition (61) provides a (possibly positive) lower bound for k. This yields the following corollary.

Corollary 15

Let the assumptions of Theorem 11 hold, and let n=1n=1 in (23). If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \ge \mu $$\end{document}γ≥μ, then the EsE is LAS for all k>0k>0.

Erlang-2 distributed memory : we assume that K follows an Erlang distribution of order 2, i.e. we take n=2n=2 in (23). Thus, the equation (58) becomes62\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda ^2(\lambda +k)^2+\lambda (\gamma +\mu )(\lambda +k)^2+ k^2\gamma \delta \left( \lambda + 2\mu \right) =0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λ2(λ+k)2+λ(γ+μ)(λ+k)2+k2γδλ+2μ=0.Then, applying the Routh-Hurwitz criterion to a fourth-order polynomial (see Sect. C.2 for the details), we obtain the following result.

Proposition 16

Let the assumptions of Theorem 11 hold, and let n=2n=2 in (23). In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_+$$\end{document}k+ is the largest positive real root of\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p(k)=\tilde{A} k^3+\tilde{B}k^2+\tilde{C} k+\tilde{D}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}p(k)=A~k3+B~k2+C~k+D~,where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{A}&:=2(\gamma +\mu +\gamma \delta )>0,\\ \tilde{B}&:=4(\gamma +\mu )^2+\gamma \delta (3\gamma -5\mu -\gamma \delta ),\\ \tilde{C}&:=2(\gamma +\mu )[(\gamma +\mu )^2+\gamma \delta (\gamma -3\mu )],\\ \tilde{D}&:=2\gamma \mu \delta (\gamma +\mu )^2<0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A~:=2(γ+μ+γδ)>0,B~:=4(γ+μ)2+γδ(3γ-5μ-γδ),C~:=2(γ+μ)[(γ+μ)2+γδ(γ-3μ)],D~:=2γμδ(γ+μ)2<0,while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_-$$\end{document}k- is either 0 or a positive root of p(k)=0p(k)=0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p'(k_-)<0$$\end{document}p′(k-)<0.

Remark 10

Note that, once the epidemiological parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _{H\leftarrow M},\, \beta _{M\leftarrow H},\, \rho ,\, \gamma ,$$\end{document}βH←M,βM←H,ρ,γ, and μ μ are fixed, the thresholds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_-$$\end{document}k- and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_+$$\end{document}k+ are fully determined by δ δ, which itself depends on l, q, a, and w.

In this section, we showed that a sufficiently large delay φ φ can destabilise the EsE via a Hopf bifurcation. This suggests that self-sustained epidemic oscillations may arise even in the absence of additional mechanisms such as demography or waning immunity. Here, we restricted the analysis to Erlang-distributed memory kernels with shape parameter n=1n=1 or n=2n=2; see (23). As noted above, the analysis may be extended to more general kernels, although this would likely result in considerably more involved computations (Andò et al. 2026; Zhang et al. 2023).

Conclusions and outlook

In this paper, we investigated the impact of protective behaviour on an SIR-SI host–vector compartmental model. We focused on single-outbreak scenarios (thus neglecting host demography and waning immunity) so that the systems under study admit only the DFE as a non-trivial equilibrium. Nevertheless, we were able to characterise the transient dynamics under various modelling assumptions.

In Sect. 2, we first analysed a model in which individuals adopt a fixed behaviour (either protecting or not protecting themselves from mosquito bites). Such models have been extensively studied in the literature (Dye and Hasibeder 1986; Hasibeder and Dye 1988; Miller and Huppert 2013), where it has been shown that imperfect or partial protection strategies may increase the basic reproduction number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document}R0, thereby enhancing the risk of an outbreak. In contrast, our analysis (Proposition 2) shows that imperfect protection in a fraction of the population may decrease \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document}R0 whenever a function F(p, q), depending on the fraction p of protected individuals and on the protection leakage q, is less than the parameter l, representing the fraction of mosquito bites on non-human hosts. To the best of our knowledge, this result is new, although consistent with previous findings. For instance, in Miller et al. (2016), threshold conditions on p were derived to determine whether protective behaviour increases or decreases \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document}R0. In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document}R0 increases when protection diverts mosquito bites towards non-protected individuals (Killeen and Smith 2007; Moore et al. 2007), whereas it may decrease if protective measures force mosquitoes to spend additional time attempting to bite protected hosts, indirectly benefiting non-protected individuals. This interpretation is consistent with viewing l as a measure of the spatial separation between hosts (see Remark 1).

We then considered a model in which behavioural changes depend on information about the epidemic. In this setting, we proved (Proposition 8) that the reproduction number is smaller when individuals switch between protected and unprotected behaviour than in the case of fixed behaviour, for the same values of p and q. While this effect had been observed numerically in Demers et al. (2018), a rigorous proof of (30) was, to our knowledge, not previously available. Biologically, this result shows that the concentration of mosquito bites on non-protected individuals (see Dye and Hasibeder (1986), Miller et al. (2016), Miller and Huppert (2013)) is mitigated when individuals switch between protected and unprotected behaviour, even when such changes occur at low rates (Demers et al. 2018).

When it is assumed that the rate of behavioural changes is much faster than epidemic dynamics (a common assumption in this class of models; see Bulai et al. (2024), Della Marca et al. (2024), Poletti et al. (2009)), it is possible to separate the time scales of the two processes by applying methods from geometric singular perturbation theory. This leads to the epidemic model (38), which includes the information index while treating the host population as homogeneous. Note that Buonomo (2014) proposed an SIR–SI model similar to (38) in an endemic setting for a malaria-like infection, accounting for human behavioural responses to public health campaigns promoting the use of bed nets. In that work, the mosquito biting rate (interpreted as the human–mosquito contact rate) is assumed to depend on past prevalence through an information index, as well as on an effort function describing the actions of the PHS in the health-promotion campaign. However, the analysis in Buonomo (2014) mainly focuses on numerical results and optimal control aspects for specific choices of the information kernel, with less emphasis on a detailed analytical study of the model dynamics.

In Sect. 5 we show that the inclusion of information-induced protective behaviour may lead to prolonged epidemic waves when the adoption of protective measures effectively prevents a large portion of infections before herd immunity and seasonality take over. Indeed, a heuristic asymptotic analysis of model (38) shows that, after the initial peak, solutions rapidly approach a slow manifold along which the infected population decays very slowly. Numerical simulations confirm this behaviour, showing that an outbreak may last for a very long time, even in the absence of host demography and recruitment of new susceptibles. Moreover, numerical simulations indicate that convergence to this slow manifold persists even when the assumption of a clear separation of time scales is relaxed. This suggests that prolonged outbreaks are an intrinsic feature of information-dependent behavioural models. We stress, however, that the model assumes constant parameters; in realistic settings, seasonal effects (e.g., temperature-driven mosquito dynamics) may significantly shorten the duration of an outbreak. Note, moreover, that in the numerical simulations we assumed that the switching functions a(J) and w(J) are linear, while non-linear functions might be more realistic.

Based on our analytical and numerical results, several extensions of the model can be considered. A natural first step would be to include demography and/or loss of immunity in the human population, which would allow for the existence of an Endemic Equilibrium under standard assumptions on the parameters. Moreover, it is important to stress that lifelong immunity upon recovery is not universal for vector-borne diseases: an individual may contract malaria multiple times during their lifetime; there exist several strains of dengue, so that reinfection by a heterologous dengue serotype can give rise to antibody-dependent enhancement, possibly inducing complex dynamics (Katzelnick et al. 2017). Including loss of immunity and/or multiple viral strains to our model would increase its realism, however with a non-negligible toll on its analytical tractability. Another possible extension is to incorporate seasonality, for instance by accounting for seasonal fluctuations in the mosquito population (e.g., due to temperature and humidity); see, for instance, Rocha et al. (2016). However, in this setting, even the computation of the BRN and CRN becomes more involved, as they are defined as the spectral radius of an infinite-dimensional next-generation operator rather than of a next-generation matrix (NGM) (Bacaër and Guernaoui 2006; Inaba 2019), and typically require numerical approximations; see, e.g., Bacaër (2007), Breda et al. (2026). Additionally, it would be interesting to investigate models evolving on three time scales: a fast one associated with the spread of information, an intermediate one corresponding to disease dynamics, and a slow one related to human demography and/or loss of immunity.

Finally, we note that individual behaviour depends on personal opinions about the infection, which may in turn be influenced by information on the epidemic as well as by social interactions. Several authors have recently investigated the interplay between opinion dynamics and epidemic spread through mathematical models (Albi et al. 2025; Chang et al. 2025; Tyson et al. 2020). Without explicitly introducing a variable describing opinion, an extension of the behavioural model (20) might be obtained by including imitation-dynamics terms of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _P(J)H_{NP}p$$\end{document}θP(J)HNPp and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _{NP}(J)H_{P}(1-p)$$\end{document}θNP(J)HP(1-p), which describe a contagion of ideas among host individuals (Bauch 1573; Manfredi and d’Onofrio 2013; Wang et al. 2016). Such contagion may be driven by individuals’ knowledge of past epidemics (Della Marca et al. 2024), or by payoff considerations (Poletti et al. 2009). Accordingly, the equation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_P$$\end{document}HP (and correspondingly for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{NP}$$\end{document}HNP) takes the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_P'=\left[ a(J) + \theta _P(J)p\right] H_{NP} - \left[ w(J)+\theta _{NP}(J)(1-p)\right] H_{P}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HP′=a(J)+θP(J)pHNP-w(J)+θNP(J)(1-p)HP.In the context of vector-borne epidemics, similar approaches have been adopted in Asfaw et al. (2018) and, more recently, in Laxmi and Bhattacharyya (2022) to model the effect of bed-net usage in malaria-like diseases. The latter, in particular, focuses on both the use and misuse of Insecticide-Treated Nets (ITNs). Both works deal with endemic scenarios: the former is based on imitation game dynamics with information-free behavioural changes, while the latter derives a related model in which behavioural changes are driven by payoff considerations linked to infection risk, which may depend on information about current prevalence, mosquito density, and seasonal effects (e.g., periodic replacement of bed nets). While Asfaw et al. (2018) provide analytical results on the model dynamics, Laxmi and Bhattacharyya (2022) mainly focus on reproduction numbers (and related considerations on optimal ITN usage) and numerical simulations, while also showing that information-induced imitation dynamics may generate recurrent epidemic waves. Interestingly, Laxmi and Bhattacharyya (2022) also discuss applications to malaria control in several African countries. In the present work, we did not consider such mechanisms. Nevertheless, the approach developed here could be extended to models incorporating evolutionary game-like dynamics, although this would inevitably lead to more involved computations (see, e.g., Della Marca et al. (2024)). This will be the subject of future work, together with the inclusion of additional factors relevant to the dynamics and control of mosquito-borne epidemics, and applications of the proposed behavioural models to data-informed real-world scenarios.
